### 1) [2611.14332](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14332v1): Tracing the Epoch of Reionization through extreme emission line galaxies around Cosmic Noon

<p> M. Delgado Mancheño, J. Gallego, S. Pascual, M. Tang, R. Pello, N. Laporte, A. Camps-Fariña, M. Chamorro-Cazorla </p>
<p>
Star-forming galaxies at z&gt;6 are thought to be the main drivers of cosmic reionization, but their faintness makes detailed study challenging even with JWST. Extreme Emission Line Galaxies (EELGs) are compact, low-mass systems with intense nebular emission and are potential lower-redshift analogues of this population. We aim to assemble and characterize a statistically significant sample of EELGs in the CANDELS/EGS field around cosmic noon, and to assess their validity as analogues of the galaxies that drove reionization. We select EELG candidates from CANDELS photometry with a J-band color-excess criterion that identifies both Hβ+[OIII] and Hα emitters. Physical properties are taken from CANDELS data products, and the selection is validated with MMT/MMIRS, Keck/MOSFIRE, HST/WFC3 and JWST/NIRSpec spectroscopy, complemented by our own GTC/OSIRIS+ program. We identify 102 Hβ+[OIII] emitters at z~1.7 and 127 Hα emitters at z~1.0. They are compact (~1.6 kpc), low-mass (log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)~8) and strongly star-forming (sSFR ~10^-8 yr^-1). Estimated purities of 84.3±1.5&percnt; (Hβ+[OIII]) and 76.8±2.0&percnt; (Hα) agree with spectroscopic confirmation rates (85&percnt; and 63&percnt;), with no evidence of significant AGN contamination. A joint Bayesian fit, corrected for completeness via a truncated likelihood, yields log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) = (6.71±0.16) + (3.31±0.46)log(1+z), suggesting an evolution in the characteristic mass of the sample with cosmic time. We spectroscopically confirm a highly pure, AGN-free sample of cosmic-noon EELGs. While their mass scale evolves with redshift, their properties consistently match those of z&gt;5 JWST extreme emitters, supporting them as intermediate-redshift analogues.
</p>

### 2) [2611.13835](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13835v1): Tracing early galaxies back in time: implications for the dearth of galaxies beyond z~15

<p> L. Ciesla, C. Carvajal-Bohorquez, D. Elbaz, G. Aufort, O. Ilbert, S. Finkelstein, A. M. Koekemoer, P. Oesch, M. Boquien, C. Gruppioni, N. Laporte </p>
<p>
Although the James Webb Space Telescope has pushed the observational frontier to z~14, the onset of star formation in the earliest galaxies remains beyond direct reach, making the star formation histories (SFH) of observed galaxies a key tool for probing earlier epochs. We develop and validate a new SFH model based on an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, capable of reproducing the bursty star formation expected in high-redshift galaxies. Tested on the SPHINX^20 mock catalogs, it accurately recovers stellar masses and SFRs and reconstructs cumulative SFHs at population level; the formation redshift is well constrained at z≳9 but not at z~8. Applied to 400 galaxies at 9&lt;z&lt;15 from the UNICORN/GOODS-South sample, we find that sources observed at z&lt;10 typically reached 20% of their stellar mass no earlier than z~12, disfavoring a clear evolutionary link to spectroscopically confirmed galaxies at z≳13. Among 218 galaxies at 10&lt;z&lt;15, only 28 had formed 20% of their stellar mass by z=15, with a median formation redshift z_form,20&percnt;=16.9^+1.1_-1.1, and 24% of our sample had already reached 10^7M<sub>&odot;</sub> by z=15. At z=17, only 11 of these 28 galaxies had reached 20% of their stellar mass. The derived survey areas required to detect them are consistent with or slightly more optimistic than previous constraints at the bright end, and larger than some literature estimates at the faint end. At z=20, a single galaxy had formed 20% of its stellar mass, yielding a tentative constraint of one object at M_UV=-18.1±0.6 per ~200 arcmin<sup>2</sup>. Our results suggest a relatively slow decline in star formation activity between z~15 and z~17, followed by a more rapid decline up to z~20.
</p>

### 3) [2611.13116](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13116v1): Revealing Hidden Excitation in the Core of NGC 4696: New Infrared Diagnostic Diagrams with JWST

<p> Olivia R Pereira, Julie Hlavacek-Larrondo, Michael Reefe, Gary J Ferland, Hyunseop Choi, Mathieu Marquis, Jorge Barrera-Ballesteros, Benjamin Vigneron, Ming Sun, Rebecca E A Canning, Gregory Taylor, Loic Albert, Francesco D'Eugenio, Megan Donahue, Andrew C Fabian, John S Gallagher, Marie-Lou Gendron-Marsolais, Pierre Guillard, Minghao Guo, Nina Hatch, Ralf Kotulla, Yuan Li, Roberto Maiolino, Allison Man, Michael A McDonald, Brian R McNamara, Valeria Olivares, Marine Prunier, Christopher S Reynolds, Carter Rhea, Annabelle Richard-Laferriere, Helen R Russell, Philippe Salome, Prathamesh Tamhane, Auriane Thilloy, Grant R Tremblay, G Mark Voit, Stephen A Walker </p>
<p>
The warm molecular and ionized gas in filamentary nebulae of cool-core BCGs provide a sensitive probe of the poorly understood exchange of energy between the ICM, cold molecular gas, and AGN feedback. Optical line ratios commonly yield similar LINER-like classifications across these systems, but do not resolve how the excitation of warm molecular gas varies between compact nuclear structures and surrounding filaments. We present JWST/NIRSpec integral-field spectroscopy of the central 3''×3'' (620×620~pc^2) of the Centaurus cluster BCG, NGC~4696. The NIR data resolve HI and ro-vibrational H<sub>2</sub> emission across compact nuclear apertures, a circumnuclear swirl, and filamentary structures. H<sub>2</sub> excitation diagrams yield characteristic low-excitation temperatures of T_ex≃1.8-2.2×10^3~K, while curvature at higher energies requires hotter or non-thermal components. The H<sub>2</sub>/Paα balance varies strongly: filamentary regions have H<sub>2</sub> 1-0 S(1)/Paα ratios 4-8 times higher than the compact nuclear apertures. In contrast, matched-aperture optical diagnostic diagrams show substantially less regional separation, with all regions occupying a low-ionization locus. We thus introduce new spatially resolved NIR diagnostic diagrams that reveal distinct molecular excitation conditions across the nucleus, swirl, and filaments. Ratios are broadly consistent with contributions from AGN-related photoionization, ICM-related cooling radiation, and non-radiative heating, although the present data do not uniquely determine their relative importance. These results demonstrate how spatially resolved NIR spectroscopy reveals excitation variations hidden in conventional optical classifications, providing a new route to constrain the multiphase cooling-feedback cycle in cluster cores.
</p>

### 4) [2611.12861](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12861v1): Little Red Dots from Primordial Black Holes: Non-Gaussianity and Accretion Evade μ-Distortion Constraints

<p> Shyam Balaji, Bryce Cyr, Sarah R. Geller, David I. Kaiser, Dario L. Lorenzoni, Evan McDonough, Wenzer Qin </p>
<p>
Recent JWST observations of "Little Red Dots" (LRDs) at z ≳ 4 suggest the existence of early supermassive black holes (SMBHs) with masses ≳ 10^6 M<sub>&odot;</sub>. Their rapid emergence challenges conventional seeding and growth scenarios based on accretion and mergers of Population III stellar remnants, motivating renewed interest in primordial black holes (PBHs) formed from primordial perturbations. Explaining such massive seeds requires power spectrum enhancements that are constrained by cosmic microwave background (CMB) spectral distortions. In this work we perform the first comprehensive Bayesian exploration of non-Gaussian primordial perturbations capable of producing SMBH seeds while remaining consistent with CMB spectral distortion constraints, and examine the role of accretion history in constraining these models. We perform a suite of Markov chain Monte Carlo analyses of power spectrum enhancements (lognormal and cut-power-law) with varying local non-Gaussianity and accretion histories, satisfying the COBE/FIRAS μ-distortion constraint with and without the requirement that the model produces SMBH seeds. We find that both models can seed LRDs and evade μ-distortion bounds for |f_NL|= O(1) and peak amplitude A= O(10^-3), when standard Eddington-limited accretion is also incorporated. Interestingly, the results exhibit a preference for both positive and negative values of f_NL. The preference for negative f_NL is strongly dependent on the accretion history, and is removed if the black holes have a small duty cycle, f_duty≲ 0.1. These results demonstrate that the JWST high-z LRD population can be seeded by primordial perturbations while remaining compatible with CMB data, and highlight the importance of next-generation CMB spectral distortion missions to test this hypothesis.
</p>

### 5) [2611.12284](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12284v1): A Comprehensive Analysis of the Hα Luminosity Functions at z~ 1.3 and z~ 2.0 in the CANUCS Lensing Fields with JWST NIRISS

<p> Qianqiao Zhou, Yuxuan Pang, Xin Wang, Karl Glazebrook, Shengzhe Wang, Hang Zhou, Pengfei Ren, Shamuhawu Hailanhazi, Peter J. Watson, Maruša Bradač, Nicha Leethochawalit, Matthew A. Malkan, Themiya Nanayakkara, Xue-Bing Wu, Hu Zhan </p>
<p>
We present Hα luminosity function (LF) measurements at redshifts z~1.3 and z~2.0. We adopt JWST grism data in five lensing cluster fields and their parallel fields from the CANUCS program: ABELL-370, MACS-J0416, MACS-J0417, MACS-J1149, MACS-J1423. Based on emission lines spectroscopically identified in the F115W, F150W and F200W filters, our final sample consists of 762 Hα emitters, including 433 at 1.1&lt;z&lt;1.5 and 329 at 1.8&lt;z&lt;2.2. Through detailed effective volume and completeness analysis for each source, we construct the Hα LF in two redshift bins. Thanks to the sensitivity of NIRISS WFSS and gravitational lensing magnification, our sample reaches intrinsic Hα luminosities down to L_Hα~10^40.6~erg~s<sup>-1</sup> at z~1.3 and L_Hα~10^41~erg~s<sup>-1</sup> at z~2.0 with completeness larger than 80&percnt;. The larger survey volume and coverage of multiple independent sightlines help mitigate cosmic variance. After accounting for cosmic variance, we constrain the faint-end slopes to be α_Hα=-1.51^+0.06_-0.04 at z~1.3 and -1.42^+0.09_-0.06 at z~2.0, indicating a mild evolution between the two redshift bins. We find a negligible contribution from bright active galactic nuclei in our sample. We estimate integrated cosmic star formation rate densities of 0.103^+0.008_-0.007~M<sub>&odot;</sub>~yr^-1~Mpc^-3 at z~1.3 and 0.149^+0.016_-0.013~M<sub>&odot;</sub>~yr^-1~Mpc^-3 at z~2.0. The emission-line samples presented here will enable further detailed studies of galaxy properties including metallicities. The methodology presented here can be readily applicable to other JWST slitless spectroscopic datasets and future wide-field slitless surveys, including those from Euclid, Roman, and the Chinese Space Station Telescope.
</p>

### 6) [2611.12282](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12282v1): A Self-Interacting Dark-Matter Route to Supermassive Kerr Black Holes via Super-Eddington Bondi Accretion: Mass-Spin Evolution

<p> Chian-Shu Chen, Feng-Li Lin </p>
<p>
Recent JWST observations motivate mechanisms for rapid black-hole growth in the early Universe. Extending our previous Schwarzschild study, we show that critical relativistic Bondi accretion of repulsively self-interacting dark matter remains an efficient super-Eddington channel for Kerr black holes with rotating inflow. Within a stationary equatorial quasi-spherical framework, smooth critical solutions exist over a finite range of inflow angular momentum set by the Kerr marginally bound capture values. Rotation changes the sonic structure and accretion efficiency, while the critical density cancellation and dark-sector/halo matching retain their Schwarzschild form. The resulting rapid growth persists until the finite halo supply becomes limiting. Because the inflow also carries angular momentum, stellar-mass seeds can grow and spin up simultaneously; illustrative population evolutions therefore provide a joint mass&ndash;spin route to early supermassive Kerr black holes.
</p>

### 7) [2611.11078](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11078v1): Studying the Fullerene Nest: JWST Observations of the Planetary Nebula Tc 1. III. Tracing Fullerene-Linked Dust Variation

<p> Simon Van Schuylenbergh, Jan Cami, Els Peeters, Morgan M. Giese, Charmi Bhatt, Dries Van De Putte, Cedric Baerts, Michael J. Barlow, Jeronimo Bernard-Salas, Alessandra Candian, P. Bryan Changala, Nick L. J. Cox, Harriet L. Dinerstein, Vincent J. Esposito, D. A. García-Hernández, Marco A. Gómez-Muñoz, Kay Justtanont, Kathleen E. Kraemer, Eric Lagadec, Arturo Manchado, Ana Monreal Ibero, Raghvendra Sahai, Ameek Sidhu, G. C. Sloan, N. C. Sterling, A. G. G. M. Tielens, Jeremy R. Walsh, Roger Wesson, Joshua Cole Whitman, Albert Zijlstra </p>
<p>
We have observed the fullerene-rich planetary nebula Tc 1 with the MIRI/MRS on JWST. The mid-infrared spectra of this spatially resolved source reveal that the dust emission is not co-spatial with the fullerene emission. Three broad features can be distinguished: a 6&ndash;9 μm plateau, a 9&ndash;13 μm plateau and the 30 μm feature. The 6&ndash;9 μm plateau does not change shape spectrally across the entire field of view, indicating it is produced by stochastically heated material. Spectral variations of the 9&ndash;13 μm plateau reveal at least three distinct components, peaking at 9.5 μm, 11 μm and 12 μm respectively. SiC produces most likely at least the 11 μm component and we find evidence that the 12 μm component might be produced by SiC grains too. The variation in relative strength of these components across the field of view hints at dust processing taking place in Tc 1. The 9.5 μm component of the 9&ndash;13 μm plateau has a strong correlation with the SiC component(s) and is thus likely produced by similar material.
</p>

### 8) [2611.10876](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10876v1): Studying the Fullerene Nest: JWST Observations of the Planetary Nebula Tc 1. II. A Spectroscopic and Spatial Study of C_60 and C_70 Emission

<p> M. M. Giese, J. Cami, S. Van Schuylenbergh, E. Peeters, C. Bhatt, D. Van De Putte, C. Baerts, M. J. Barlow, J. Bernard-Salas, A. Candian, P. B. Changala, N. L. J. Cox, H. L. Dinerstein, V. J. Esposito, D. A. García-Hernández, M. A. Gómez-Muñoz, K. Justtanont, K. E. Kraemer, E. Lagadec, A. Manchado, A. Monreal Ibero, R. Sahai, A. Sidhu, G. C. Sloan, N. C. Sterling, A. G. G. M. Tielens, J. R. Walsh, R. Wesson, J. C. Whitman, A. Zijlstra, L. Finazzi, J. Martens, G. Berden, J. Oomens </p>
<p>
We present a detailed analysis of the fullerene emission bands in JWST MIRI/MRS observations of the planetary nebula Tc 1. The observations reveal the emission features due to neutral C_60 and C_70 in unprecedented detail. The combined fullerene emission represents ~2.6% of the total infrared output, suggesting that ~0.6% of the carbon budget is locked up in fullerenes. We find no clear evidence for C_60^+, C_70^+ or C_60^- in the spectrum. The high spectral resolution allows us to separate the fullerene bands from atomic emission lines for the first time, allowing for a much better characterization of the band profiles and yielding more accurate band ratios than previous observations. We see no spatial variation in the individual C_60 and C_70 band profile shapes across all pixels. The 17.4 and 18.9 μm C_60 bands show clear resolved ro-vibrational structure in their profiles, and similar structured emission is seen in the 12.5, 14.8, 17.7, and 21.8 μm C_70 bands, further establishing that all emitting fullerene molecules are in the gas phase. We report a higher 7.0/18.9 μm C_60 band ratio (~1.4-1.9) than previously reported (~1.3). We note that all bands, except the 8.5 μm band, contain a significant contribution from C_70, complicating the analysis. The fullerene emission is spatially concentrated in a thin, asymmetric shell at a radius of 5-6'' from the central star. The shell lies at the outer edge of the core of the nebula, which was ejected in a single, brief episode ≳4,000 years ago.
</p>

### 9) [2611.10458](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10458v1): B-fields In STar-forming Region Observations (BISTRO): Population-Level Universality and Environmental Structuring of 450/850 μm Polarization Signatures

<p> Ray S. Furuya, David Berry, Pierre Bastien, Tetsuo Hasegawa, Masafumi Matsumura, Thiem Hoang, Lapo Fanciullo, Kazunari Iwasaki, Simon Coudé, Takashi Onaka, Pham Ngoc Diep, Sarah Sadavoy, Hua-bai Li, Koji Kawabata, Doris Arzoumanian, Yasuo Doi, Jia-Wei Wang, Tao-Chung Ching, Janik Karoly, Jihye Hwang, Glenn J. White, A-Ran Lyo, Kohji Tomisaka, Le Ngoc Tram, Frédérick Poidevin, Valentin J. M. Le Gouellec, Hiroko Shinnaga, Masato I. N. Kobayashi, Junhao Liu, Mehrnoosh Tahani, Nguyen Bich Ngoc, Jennifer Hatchell, Archana Soam, Hsi-Wei Yen, Chakali Eswaraiah, Vera Könyves, Jintai Wu, Jeong-Eun Lee, Yusuke Tsukamoto, Ekta Sharma, Tie Liu, Qilao Gu, Hiroyuki Nakanishi, Tsuyoshi Inoue, Fumitaka Nakamura, Rachel Friesen, Jean-François Robitaille, Yoshiaki Misugi, Raiga Kashiwagi, Per Friberg, Sarah Graves, Mark Rawlings, Harriet Parsons, David Eden, Huei-Ru Vivien Chen, Najimudeen Bijas, Motohide Tamura, Tyler L. Bourke, Nagayoshi Ohashi, Woojin Kwon, Jongsoo Kim, Shih-Ping Lai, Keping Qiu, Di Li, Shu-ichiro Inutsuka, Kate Pattle, Derek Ward-Thompson, BISTRO Collaboration </p>
<p>
We observed 69 molecular cloud regions at 450 and 850 μm with the POL-2 polarimeter on the James Clerk Maxwell Telescope and matched the maps to a common 12.6'' beam. Polarization fractions are typically lower at 450 μm. The median of 30 measured ratios is P_450/P_850 = 0.69 ± 0.02_sys ± 0.03_stat. Across 35 regions with reliable spectral indices of total (α) and polarized (γ) intensity, the medians over sources are &langle;α&rangle; = 3.33 and &langle;γ&rangle; = 2.85-3.04. The median γ- α is -0.57 to -0.45, with γ&lt; α in 24-27 regions. These ranges span the two extreme placements of 3σ limits for polarized intensity below the noise floor. The position angles at the two wavelengths agree within 10&deg; in 10 of 13 regional medians, disfavoring a geometric origin that would rotate the angles. The medians of γ-α in 9 low-mass, 19 high-mass and 7 Central Molecular Zone (CMZ) regions are not ordered low-mass &lt; high-mass &lt; CMZ (Jonckheere-Terpstra p = 0.43-0.70). A difference among classes is not robustly detected. The Kruskal-Wallis p = 0.056-0.078 becomes p = 0.026-0.041 when the Rosette Molecular Cloud alone is excluded, although the high-mass class has the lowest median in every treatment. The classes follow a distance sequence (the beam spans 0.008-0.51 pc), coupling environment and physical scale. Within clouds, the 450 μm polarization fraction decreases with Herschel column density in all 7 fields with sufficient dynamic range. Coefficients from pooled pixels mix trends within clouds, differences between clouds, and the influence of OMC 1. They therefore do not define a single law for individual pixels. The wavelength dependence across sources provides a benchmark for grain alignment. We cannot order it by environment and do not propose a unique mechanism.
</p>

### 10) [2611.10227](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10227v1): Local Pitch Angles of Galactic Structures in Nearby Galaxies with JWST: II. Dust Lanes and Gas Flows in Bar-Dominated Systems

<p> Stella Faria-Costa, Irapuan Rodrigues, Ivânio Puerari </p>
<p>
We present a spatially resolved study of local pitch angles in six barred spiral galaxies observed by PHANGS&ndash;JWST, focusing on bar-dominated regions where dust lanes are the main structures. We combine a two-dimensional Fourier transform (2D-FT) with a circular-window correlation method to map pitch angles across multiple wavelengths and spatial scales, extending our previous analysis of spiral arms and interarm substructures in disk-dominated galaxies. The dust lanes show relatively large pitch angles, typically |α|≃35&deg;&ndash;70&deg;, well above those measured for spiral arms. In several galaxies, the pitch angle changes systematically along the dust lane, with smaller values in the inner regions and larger values farther along the bar, tracing variations in local curvature. The multi-scale analysis shows that larger windows recover the overall geometry of the dust lanes, whereas smaller windows reveal local irregularities and secondary structures. MIRI images generally provide the clearest view of the dust morphology and allow a more precise characterization of local pitch-angle variations, while NIRCam offers a complementary view of the underlying stellar distribution. Unlike spiral-arm pitch angles, which are commonly associated with density-wave patterns, the values measured here characterize the local orientation and curvature of dust lanes associated with bar-driven gas flows. Together with the results of our companion study of disk-dominated galaxies, these findings support a unified, scale-dependent framework in which local pitch angles serve as observational diagnostics of distinct dynamical regimes in nearby galaxies.
</p>

### 11) [2611.10222](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10222v1): Local Pitch Angles of Galactic Structures in Nearby Galaxies with JWST: I. Spiral Arms and Interarm Substructures in Disk-Dominated Systems

<p> Stella Faria-Costa, Irapuan Rodrigues, Ivânio Puerari </p>
<p>
We present a spatially resolved study of spiral arms and feather substructures in seven nearby galaxies observed with JWST. Using eight images per galaxy, from four NIRCam and four MIRI filters, we combine a two-dimensional Fourier transform (2D-FT) with a circular-window correlation method to measure both global and local pitch angles. The 2D-FT identifies the dominant azimuthal mode and the corresponding p_max, allowing us to reconstruct the spiral pattern and define arm and interarm masks. We then apply cosine filters with different pitch angles and window sizes in the (\ln (R),θ) plane to map the pitch-angle distribution across multiple spatial scales. NIRCam images provide the most robust measurements of the main spiral pattern, whereas MIRI data reveal dusty interarm features, particularly feathers, with greater contrast at the smallest window size (D_w=0.25). Across the sample, the main arms typically have |α|≃15&deg;&ndash;30&deg;, while feathers show systematically larger values, often above 50&deg;. These results demonstrate that pitch angle is scale dependent and that different structural components within the same galaxy can occupy distinct angular regimes. NIRCam and MIRI therefore offer complementary views of spiral structure, with the former tracing the large-scale stellar backbone and the latter exposing finer dust substructures. Together with the companion study of barred galaxies, this work provides a common framework for using local pitch angles to investigate distinct dynamical environments in nearby galaxies.
</p>

### 12) [2611.10125](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10125v1): First detection of suprathermal CH^+ rotational emissions from chemical pumping: JWST/MIRI observations of the HH 168 molecular shock

<p> David A. Neufeld, Joel D. Green, Dan M. Watson, Paule Sonnentrucker </p>
<p>
The methylidyne cation forms in warm or shocked interstellar gas through the endothermic reaction C^+ + H<sub>2</sub> -&gt; CH^+ + H. Because C^+, which is common in irradiated and shocked gas, reacts with H<sub>2</sub> only when the reactants supply &gt; 0.4 eV, CH^+ traces energy deposition in molecular gas. JWST/MIRI observations of HH 168 reveal the pure rotational lines R(17)-R(31) of CH^+ at 13.7-21.3 micron, from upper levels J = 18-32 (0.57-1.65 eV). Eleven lines exceed 5 sigma in both of the two fields that we observed and match the hot H<sub>2</sub> in radial velocity and spatial distribution. Because collisional excitation is negligible, each ion entering the v = 0 ladder emits one photon in every R(J) line below its entry level, so the line intensities provide the first measured rotational distribution of the ground-state products. The photon intensity falls almost linearly with increasing J, as expected for formation at a similar rate into every level up to J ~ 33; formation in J = 32 requires &gt; 2 eV of reactant energy. The line positions, up to 150 km/s redward of ExoMol predictions, provide the first measurement of the CH^+ rotational intervals above J = 16. A Boltzmann distribution at φT ~ 6200 K, where φ is the fraction of the available energy appearing in rotation, fits slightly better, implying reactant energies equivalent to several thousand kelvin. These lines probe the dynamics of a prototypical interstellar ion-molecule reaction.
</p>

### 13) [2611.10122](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10122v1): First detection of the forbidden rotational spectrum of H_3^+: JWST/MIRI observations of the shocked gas in HH 168

<p> David A. Neufeld, Joel D. Green, Dan M. Watson, Paule Sonnentrucker </p>
<p>
The H_3^+ molecular ion is the cornerstone of interstellar ion-molecule chemistry. Its vibrational transitions have long been observed, in absorption along many interstellar sight lines and in emission from the upper atmospheres of the giant planets, but its pure rotational spectrum, which consists only of "forbidden" transitions made possible by centrifugal distortion, had not been observed in the laboratory or in any astronomical source. With JWST/MIRI we have detected this spectrum toward the Herbig-Haro object HH 168 in Cepheus A West. Twenty-six lines are detected above 5 σ in one or both of two fields, of which the (J,K) = (5,0) -&gt; (4,3) line at 16.325 μm is the strongest. The H_3^+ lines share the velocity and spatial distribution of the hot shocked H<sub>2</sub>, not of the ionized gas. A rotation diagram yields a rotational temperature of ~ 730 K, but the level populations are not thermal and are best explained by subthermal excitation in gas at &gt; 2000 K. We compare the observations with models of shocks propagating into mildly irradiated molecular gas. Constrained by the H<sub>2</sub> rotational lines, a dissociative J-type shock predicts H_3^+ emission within a factor ~1.5 of that observed, while a nondissociative C-type shock, which fits the H<sub>2</sub> lines better, underpredicts it by a factor 5-6, although the excitation of H_3^+ by energetic collisions is poorly known. Archival MIRI data show the H_3^+ forbidden rotational spectrum in the HH 211 bow shock. The H_3^+ lines provide a new probe of the ionization in shocked molecular gas.
</p>

### 14) [2611.09875](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09875v1): SN 2026ahvu in NGC 1433 May Be a Prompt Type Ia Supernova with a Delay Time Under 100 Myr

<p> Sumit K. Sarbadhicary, Kiana Henny, Adam Leroy, Karin Sandstrom, Kirsten L. Larson, David A. Thilker, Gagandeep S. Anand, Ashley T. Barnes, Laura A. Lopez, Daniel Maschmann, Marco Troncoso, Hwihyun Kim, Janice C. Lee, Frank Bigiel, Médéric Boquien, Rupali Chandar, Ryan Chown, Daniel A. Dale, Sinan Deger, Oleg V. Egorov, Simon C. O. Glover, Oleg Y. Gnedin, Jay González Lobos, Hao He, Rebecca J. Houghton, Debosmita Pathak, M. Jimena Rodriguez, Eva Schinnerer, Michael Tucker, Tony D. Weinbeck, Thomas G. Williams, Bradley C. Whitmore, Leonardo Ubeda </p>
<p>
SN 2026ahvu is a Type Ia supernova (SN) that occurred in the nearby galaxy NGC 1433. Using archival Hubble and JWST imaging from the PHANGS survey, we show that the SN lies within 0.19'' and 0.26'' of two UV-bright star clusters. This 0.19/0.26'' separation corresponds to 8/11 pc (17/24 pc) at the reported distances of 9.1 and 18.9 Mpc, respectively. The probability of finding an unrelated Type Ia SN this close to a cataloged star cluster by chance is only ~0.4 %. Thus, SN 2026ahvu is likely the first Type Ia SN that can be associated with relatively young star clusters in a nearby galaxy. From spectral energy distribution fitting using HST+JWST (or HST-only) data, we find that the clusters have age estimates of 37_-5^+3 (57_-9^+14) Myr and 95_-13^+15 (88_-12^+14) Myr. Association with either cluster would mean that SN 2026ahvu should be considered a prompt SN Ia and provide one of the most concrete measurements to date of a prompt Ia delay time. The median age of the younger star cluster is close to the minimum possible delay time for SN Ia progenitors. If the SN is indeed associated with the younger cluster, it would imply that Type Ia SNe can occur almost immediately after white dwarf formation. If the SN is associated with the older cluster, this discovery still presents the first example of a prompt Type Ia SN with delay time under 100 Myr.
</p>

### 15) [2611.09397](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09397v1): Where the red rise stops: JWST/MIRI reveals a near-infrared turnover in the Little Red Dot Abell2744-QSO1

<p> E. Iani, P. Rinaldi, A. Torralba, J. Matthee, F. D'Eugenio, A. Gupta, S. Mascia, C. Di Cesare, G. Oestlin, G. Kotiwale, M. Catone, G. Rodighiero </p>
<p>
Little Red Dots (LRDs) are compact sources with a blue rest-frame UV, a steep red optical continuum and, often, broad hydrogen lines. They have been interpreted as dense stellar systems, dust-reddened active galactic nuclei, or black holes embedded in dense, dust-poor gas, scenarios that are hard to tell apart in the rest-frame UV and optical. We present JWST/MIRI F770W and F1000W imaging of the triply lensed Abell2744-QSO1 at z=7.04, probing rest-frame 0.96 and 1.24um. QSO1 is unresolved, and its steep optical rise stops near rest-frame 1um. Up to 1.24um, a T~4450K blackbody, or a hotter one reddened by dust, describes the continuum, with no sign of the hot dust seen in most type-1 quasars. Our statistically preferred SED fits produce the red continuum with (3-4)x10^9 M<sub>&odot;</sub> of stars, over 100 times the dynamical limit on the host mass, and are therefore disfavoured. An accretion disc in a dense gas cocoon respects this limit but over-predicts the 1.24um flux by 5 sigma. Super-Eddington accretion models with a thermalising envelope reproduce the turnover, but only for a black hole ~2.5dex less massive than the dynamical estimate. For a dust-reddened accretion disc (A_V ~ 2 mag), dust at ~1200K can re-emit at most ~2 per cent of the absorbed luminosity, and most of it would have to emerge at ~70-600K, unless the dust covers only a small fraction of the sky. The dynamics favour an accretion-powered source, but our data cannot distinguish dust reddening from processing by dense gas. MIRI imaging at &gt;18um could separate these scenarios.
</p>

### 16) [2611.08676](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08676v1): Expanding the Volatile Abundance Inventory for High-Priority K-Dwarf JWST Exoplanet Systems

<p> David R. Coria, Neda Hejazi, Ian J. M. Crossfield, Ismael Mireles, Casey L. Brinkman, Jared R. Kolecki, Emilio Marfil, Thomas Nordlander, Lauren M. Weiss, Diana Dragomir </p>
<p>
There are exceedingly few chemical abundance measurements available in the literature for K dwarf stars, especially for those with T_eff &lt; 4700 K due to an incompatibility of traditional analyses with their molecular-line-ridden optical spectra. To address the need for K dwarf abundance constraints, we present an elemental ([C/H], [O/H]) and isotopic (^12C/^13C, ^16O/^18O) abundance analysis for six K dwarf exoplanet host stars: K2-141, NGTS-10, WASP-43, WASP-69, WASP-80, and HATS-72. Each system hosts an exoplanet targeted by JWST characterization efforts, making host star abundances especially critical as baseline references. Using MARCS model atmospheres, the TurboSpectrum spectral synthesis code, and near-infrared IGRINS spectra, we derive novel [C/H] and [O/H] abundance ratios for these high-priority K dwarf host stars. We further investigate prospects for determining ^12C/^13C and ^16O/^18O ratios using ^13C^16O and ^12C^18O lines from the 2.3 micron CO bandhead. The observed spectra are too noisy to measure ^16O/^18O ratios for the sample, but we do find sub-solar ^12C/^13C ratios for five out of six targets. We also derive uniform gyrochronological ages for each host star to compare with galactic chemical evolution models and identify underlying abundance-age trends. NIR H+K band spectroscopy provides an alternative to optical, atomic line-derived carbon and oxygen abundances for cool dwarf stars. The growing catalog of precise host star elemental and isotopic abundances complements ongoing observations of their companions and provides a baseline for understanding their chemical composition, origin, and evolution.
</p>

### 17) [2611.08630](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08630v1): VENUS: Strong Lens Mass Models and Einstein Radii of 60 Galaxy Clusters

<p> Masamune Oguri, Vasily Kokorev, Shun Nishida, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Seiji Fujimoto, Dan Coe, Johan Richard, Jose M. Diego, Fengwu Sun, Lukas J. Furtak, Maruša Bradač, Larry D. Bradley, John Chisholm, Christopher J. Conselice, Miroslava Dessauges-Zavadsky, Henry C. Ferguson, Steven L. Finkelstein, Brenda L. Frye, Mauro González-Otero, Tiger Yu-Yang Hsiao, Kotaro Kohno, Ray A. Lucas, José M. Palencia, Richard Pan, Massimo Ricotti, Gregor Rihtaršič, Luke Robbins, Chris Willott </p>
<p>
We present the strong lens mass modeling result of 60 galaxy clusters targeted by Vast Exploration for Nascent, Unexplored Sources (VENUS), the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) multi-epoch Treasury program. We obtain refined mass models for the 60 clusters with the glafic software by fitting positions of ~ 4500 multiple images, ~ 1150 of which are newly identified multiple images with help of multi-band JWST images. Each cluster contains typically ~ 50 multiple images, thanks to the selection of target clusters based on their high lensing strengths. We validate our models with the Jackknife method. The median Einstein radius is ~ 30'' and the distribution of the Einstein radii extends out to ~ 60'' for the source redshift of 9, which confirms that their lensing strengths are indeed among the highest known to date. For the source redshift of 9, we identify MACS J0717.5+3745, MACS J0600.1-2008, and PLCK G287.0+32.9 as clusters with the largest Einstein radii as well as the largest areas of the high-magnification (|μ|&gt;10) regions. We find that the observed Einstein radii of VENUS clusters are fully compatible with the standard cold dark matter model.
</p>

### 18) [2611.08612](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08612v1): Jeans Dynamical Constraints on the IMF in Massive Quiescent Galaxies at z~0.7 from JWST/NIRSpec

<p> Martje Slob, Mariska Kriek, Chloe M. Cheng, Arjen van der Wel, Aliza G. Beverage, Rachel Bezanson, Charlie Conroy, Anna de Graaff, Elham Eftekhari, Robert Feldmann, Wout M. Goesaert, Meng Gu, Joel Leja, Brian Lorenz, Pavel E. Mancera Piña, Ignacio Martín-Navarro, Andrew B. Newman, Sedona H. Price, Alice E. Shapley, Katherine A. Suess, Jesse van de Sande, Pieter van Dokkum </p>
<p>
We present dynamical masses for nine massive quiescent galaxies at z~0.7 from the JWST-IMFERNO program, measured from ultra-deep (~32hr), spatially resolved NIRSpec/MSA spectroscopy combined with NIRCam imaging. The most massive of these galaxies have previously been found to have a bottom-heavy initial mass function (IMF), based on measurements of dwarf-sensitive absorption features. Here, we examine whether the stellar masses inferred for these IMFs are consistent with the dynamical masses of these galaxies. We derive the dynamical masses by including Jeans Anisotropic Modelling within a forward-modelling framework that accounts for instrumental optics, source morphology, and geometric effects. We compare the resulting dynamical masses to the stellar masses for different IMF assumptions. A Milky Way IMF produces physically plausible dynamical-to-stellar mass ratios but implies central dark matter fractions (f_DM,1R_e=0.54±0.06) that are a factor ~2 higher compared to local ETGs and simulations. When uniformly applying the IMF that was measured from absorption features, the mean dark matter fraction becomes low (f_DM,1R_e=0.02±0.18), and the stellar masses exceed the dynamical mass at 2σ significance for two out of seven galaxies in the sample. When we adopt the measured IMF only in the central R_e and use a MW IMF in the outskirts, we find realistic dark matter fractions (f_DM,1R_e=0.28±0.12) and consistent dynamical masses. These results support a scenario where the cores of the most massive quiescent galaxies at z~0.7 had a bottom-heavy IMF, similar to those observed in the centres of local early-type galaxies. Our results are consistent with an inside-out growth scenario, in which size and mass growth of massive galaxies proceeds after quenching through minor mergers, leaving the bottom-heavy cores mostly intact.
</p>

### 19) [2611.08594](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08594v1): Constraining Dust Attenuation Curves in Star-Forming Galaxies at Cosmic Noon Using Multiple Emission Line Ratios

<p> Owen Zafuta, Joseph Havens, Bren E. Backhaus, Allison Kirkpatrick, Gregory Troiani, Kurt Hamblin </p>
<p>
Dust attenuation remains a dominant systematic uncertainty in measurements of an emission line, and therefore galaxies properties derived from the emission lines such as star formation rates. Corrections based only on the Balmer decrement require an assumed attenuation-curve slope, even though that slope varies among galaxies. We use JWST/NIRSpec spectroscopy from JADES to constrain effective dust attenuation curves for 386 star-forming galaxies at 0.5≤ z≤7. For each galaxy, we combine Hα, Hβ, and one additional hydrogen recombination line and solve for the normalization τ_V and slope n of a power-law attenuation curve, allowing the slope to vary from galaxy to galaxy. We found slopes and normalization span a broad range even for galaxies at similar redshifts. Additionally, our method using Paschen transitions provided substantially greater constraints the attenuation-curve slope than Balmer-only combinations. When comparing to SMC-like or Calzetti-like corrections we find our method produces more consistent star formation rates with less scatter. These improved dust corrections provide an avenue to better describe star formation rates in the epoch of peak star formation and the early universe.
</p>

### 20) [2611.08346](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08346v1): The cold substellar frontier in JWST Extragalactic surveys: a robust parallax measurement yields the most distant known ultra-cold Y dwarf

<p> Feng-Yuan Frey Liu, Derek J. McLeod, Xueqing Chen, Ben J. Sutlieff, Ho-Hin Leung, Clémence Fontanive, James S. Dunlop, Ross J. McLure, Trent J. Dupuy, Adam C. Carnall, Thomas M. Stanton, Beth A. Biller, Isaac J. B. Holst, Callum T. Donnan, Ryan Begley </p>
<p>
We present an analysis of JADES-GS-BD-5, an extremely cold Y dwarf identified in the JADES/GOODS-South field. Using multi-epoch JWST/NIRCam imaging spanning a ≃2-yr baseline, we calibrate the JWST astrometry and obtain the first robust parallax measurement for a brown dwarf discovered in a JWST extragalactic deep field. We measure a proper motion of μ_tot=49.8^+0.3_-0.3 mas yr^-1 and a parallax of \varpi=15.7^+1.4_-1.3 mas, corresponding to a geometric distance of d=64^+6_-5 pc, ~3× further than that of the previously known most distant Y dwarfs with parallax-measured distances. We hence determine a tangential velocity of v_tan=15.0^+1.4_-1.3 km s<sup>-1</sup>, consistent with a Galactic thin-disk object. We find no evidence for binary acceleration, significant photometric variability, or a plausible host. The J-band spectral morphology of JADES-GS-BD-5 is consistent with existing Y-dwarf templates, while its extremely faint luminosity and overall near-infrared spectral profile confirm that it is one of the coldest dwarfs discovered to date. We therefore adopt a classification of &gt;Y1, consistent with a NIRCam colour-based proxy of cY1.5. The robust parallax-determined distance allows us to determine its bolometric luminosity, log(L_bol/L<sub>&odot;</sub>)=-7.08^+0.09_-0.09. Evolutionary modelling places JADES-GS-BD-5 in the planetary-mass regime and makes it a rare benchmark for the study of cold giant-planet atmospheres. Current atmospheric models fail to reproduce its SED in detail, pointing to the importance of disequilibrium chemistry and vertical mixing. Finally, we also report additional proper motion measurements for eight of the other nine known JADES Y-dwarf candidates, highlighting JWST extragalactic surveys as a powerful route to building a statistically useful, parallax-calibrated sample of Y dwarfs.
</p>

### 21) [2611.08563](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08563v1): A combined PRIMER+MINERVA study of the evolution of [O\mboxIII]+Hβ equivalent width from z=3 to z=8.5

<p> Isaac J. B. Holst, Ross J. McLure, Ryan Begley, Derek J. McLeod, James S. Dunlop, Patricia V. Fort, Stella Antonogiannaki, Cecilia Bondestam, Adam C. Carnall, Fergus Cullen, Richard Ellis, Norman A. Grogin, Anton M. Koekemoer, Ho-Hin Leung, Feng-Yuan Frey Liu, Thomas M. Stanton, Struan D. Stevenson, Elizabeth Taylor </p>
<p>
We present an analysis of the evolution of [O III]+Hβ equivalent width (W_λ) over the redshift range z=3 to z=8.5, based on combining the JWST NIRCam PRIMER and MINERVA imaging in the UDS survey field. We find that the combined medium- and broad-band data set reduces the photometric redshift outlier rate to η= 4.7&percnt; at m_F444W≤28, a 42&percnt; reduction compared with PRIMER imaging alone. We also demonstrate that the combined data set facilitates the accurate recovery of [O III]+Hβ equivalent widths in redshift bins centred on z~3.2,  4.0,  5.0,  7.2,  8.3; corresponding to the F210M, F250M, F300M, F410M and F460M filters. Based on a final sample of N=1964 galaxies with M_UV≤-19.25, we first provide new constraints on the bright end of the z=7-8 [O III]+Hβ luminosity function (log_10φ^* = -4.3_-0.5^+0.2, log_10L^* = -43.4_-0.2^+0.3), before proceeding to investigate the redshift evolution of [O III]+Hβ equivalent width. Using SED modelling based on non-parametric continuity star-formation histories, we find that the median [O III]+Hβ equivalent width monotonically increases with redshift, rising from log_10(W_λ)=2.54^+0.01_-0.01 at z≃ 3 to log_10(W_λ)=3.05^+0.03_-0.03 at z≃ 8. In tandem, the distribution of log_10(W_λ) is observed to become increasingly asymmetric with redshift, producing a fraction of extreme emission-line galaxies (EELGs, W_λ&gt;1000 Å) that reaches f_EELG=0.50±0.10 by z=8.3. Our SED modelling results indicate that the physical driver of this evolution is increasingly bursty star-formation, with EELGs the result of particularly high specific star-formation rates over the 10 Myr prior to observation.
</p>

### 22) [2611.07546](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07546v1): Violent relaxation in hierarchical stellar systems: pathways toward massive black hole formation in the early Universe

<p> Dominik R. G. Schleicher, Francesco Flammini Dotti, Matías Liempi, Tommaso Zana, Andrés Escala, Muhammad A. Latif, Roberto Capuzzo-Dolcetta, Marcelo C. Vergara, Lorenzo Paparella </p>
<p>
Massive stellar systems have been detected in the high-redshift Universe via the James Webb Space Telescope using gravitational lensing techniques. Such stellar systems may evolve on short dynamical timescales, despite a formally large 2-body relaxation time. If the stellar system consists of a large number of gravitationally bound clusters, a 2-body relaxation time can be defined for the star clusters in analogy to the 2-body relaxation time for stars, implying a faster evolution determined by the number of stars per cluster. At their formation, clusters will go through a process of "cold collapse" as part of their early, non-collisional, evolution. This phase enhances the mass segregation between the more massive and less massive population of stars. We derive a criteria based on the mass of the two components that determines under which conditions the massive component may even decouple from the cluster itself. Mergers of clusters and multiple mergers on average tend to produce systems with larger cluster radii. For the formation of massive black holes via dense stellar systems, the most promising pathway consists in the cold collapse of a dense massive cluster, after few mergers (or possibly none). In such a cluster with a sufficient number of massive stars, the massive population of stars will dynamically decouple in the central region and go through core collapse, leading to the formation of a central massive object. In the case of multiple mergers, massive but more spatially extended stellar systems will form.
</p>

Stanton, Yang Sun, Chamilla Terp, Francesco Valentino, Arjen van der Wel, Jorick S. Vink, Bingjie Wang, Vivienne Wild, Stephen M. Wilkins, Po-Feng Wu, Stefano Zibetti </p>
<p>
We argue for a significant investment of JWST time into an extremely deep medium-resolution NIRSpec MSA survey targeting a large, representative sample of high-redshift (z&gt;1.5) galaxies, specifically intended to obtain high-SNR continuum detections. This is only possible with JWST, and no planned future instrumentation has similar capabilities, meaning that obtaining such observations with JWST must be urgently prioritised. The vast majority of existing archival NIRSpec observations are not deep enough for continuum science, being focused on the detection of emission lines. Deeper continuum data opens up the ability to constrain a much wider range of key galaxy physical parameters, including stellar population ages and metallicities, stellar kinematics and neutral gas physical properties, inflows and outflows. Such measurements will provide crucial insight into quenching physics, galaxy dynamical masses, merger histories, pristine early star-formation, reionization and the physics of Little Red Dots, as well as enabling all of the emission-line science that can be achieved with shallower observations. To facilitate future survey planning, we present an empirically derived relationship between rest-optical continuum SNR in JWST NIRSpec medium-resolution data, rest-optical apparent magnitude and exposure time, which we show to be independent of redshift (and hence the grating used). We then provide a 1000-hour sketch survey plan based on achieving the continuum SNR =20/pixel necessary to constrain key physical properties and scaling relations for a large galaxy sample at z&gt;1.5. We finally discuss NIRSpec sky subtraction strategies, which are of critical importance for continuum SNR.
</p>

### 24) [2611.07064](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07064v1): The ALPINE-CRISTAL-JWST Survey: The Tully-Fisher Relation at z = 4-6

<p> Diego A. Gómez-Espinoza, Juan Molina, Edo Ibar, Manuel Aravena, Roberto J. Assef, Mederic Boquien, Matthieu Béthermin, Paolo Cassata, Elisabete da Cunha, Poulomi Dam, Miroslava Dessauges-Zavadsky, Andreas L. Faisst, Seiji Fujimoto, Jorge Gónzalez-López, Rodrigo Herrera-Camus, Hanae Inami, Anton M. Koekemoer, Lilian L. Lee, Yuan Li, L. Liu, Bahram Mobasher, Marco Palla, Monica Relano, Michael Romano, John D. Silverman, Justin Spilker, Hannah Übler, Daniela Vergani, Vicente Villanueva, Wuji Wang </p>
<p>
The Tully-Fisher relation provides a fundamental link between galaxy mass and their kinematics. Although its evolution is relatively well constrained up to cosmic noon, observational probes at higher redshifts remain scarce. In this work, we investigate the stellar and baryonic Tully-Fisher relations (TFRs) at z=4-6 by analyzing JWST/NIRSpec IFU observations of 18 main-sequence star-forming galaxies belonging to the ALPINE-CRISTAL-JWST survey. We apply a morpho-kinematic disk classification scheme, finding five disk-like galaxies and a survey disk fraction of 0.28±0.11. These disk galaxies span stellar masses of 10^9.5-10.5 M<sub>&odot;</sub>, intrinsic rotational velocities of 91-127 km s<sup>-1</sup>, and intrinsic velocity dispersions of 66-129 km s<sup>-1</sup>. For both versions of the TFR, we find that the ALPINE-CRISTAL-JWST disks lie below the local relations and are likely offset from those reported at cosmic noon (z≈2.3). By fixing the TFR slope to the z=0 value, we measure zero-point offsets for the stellar-mass TFR of -0.96±0.18 dex and -0.50±0.18 dex relative to the local Universe and cosmic noon, respectively. For the baryonic TFR, we find zero-point offsets of -0.57±0.20 dex and -0.30±0.20 dex with respect to the local Universe and cosmic noon relations. Our measurements are consistent with the redshift evolution of the zero-points of both TFRs being driven by the evolution of dark matter halo properties with cosmic time, modulated by a reduced contribution of dark matter to the dynamical mass on the galaxy scale.
</p>

Trump, Takafumi Tsukui, Jack C. Turner, Hannah Übler, Hideki Umehata, Anavi Uppal, Eros Vanzella, Alessandra Venditti, Giacomo Venturi, Enrico Veraldi, Aswin P Vijayan, Natalia C. Villanueva, Fabio Vito, Mark Vogelsberger, Marta Volonteri, Benedetta Vulcani, Fabian Walter, Feige Wang, Tao Wang, Bingjie Wang, John R. Weaver, Arjen van der Wel, Andrew Weldon, Katherine E. Wolter, Zihao Wu, Yunjing Wu, Po-Feng Wu, Mengyuan Xiao, Haojing Yan, Hiroto Yanagisawa, Jinyi Yang, Guang Yang, L. Y. Aaron Yung, Erik Zackrisson, Tommaso Zana, Anita Zanella, Zijian Zhang, Huanian Zhang, Saiyang Zhang, Hao Zhang, Haowen Zhang, Roy J. Zhao, Dazhi Zhou, Yongda Zhu, Ming-Yang Zhuang, Adi Zitrin </p>
<p>
The Genesis Initiative is an extragalactic community-wide effort to coordinate white papers in response to the STScI Multi-Cycle Treasury (MCT) concept call. Two of JWST's primary science goals are to discover and characterize the first stars and galaxies, and to understand how they evolved into the galaxies we see today - questions whose fundamental importance has been affirmed by the past three decadal surveys. JWST's first five years have surpassed expectations, revealing a bounty of unexpected discoveries about the early universe. Yet these discoveries have raised as many questions as they have answered, and key mission goals remain unmet. Transforming this excitement into a quantitative physical understanding of early galaxy formation requires a uniform, statistically controlled legacy dataset that the current portfolio of smaller, heterogeneous programs cannot provide. The history of the Hubble Space Telescope offers a clear precedent. Its most transformative extragalactic results came from large, coordinated surveys far exceeding what the standard time-allocation process typically approves, and these uniform datasets remain scientifically productive decades later. We argue that JWST is now poised for the same leap. Answering the field's central open questions demands coordinated, community-led surveys of ~1000-2000 hr, with uniform and well-characterized selection functions that are structurally impossible within single-cycle programs. Such an investment is time-critical while JWST's instruments remain at peak performance, and would maximize synergy with ALMA, Euclid, Roman, Rubin, and future facilities. We therefore urge STScI to open a MCT channel, enabling the bold, ambitious surveys needed to understand how galaxies evolve from first light to the present day.
</p>

### 26) [2611.07020](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07020v1): The obscured nucleus of NGC3256 S. I. Anisotropic AGN near-IR radiation field traced by CO v=1-0 with JWST/NIRSpec

<p> Miguel Pereira-Santaella, Eduardo González-Alfonso, Ismael García-Bernete, Andreas Efstathiou, Dimitra Rigopoulou, Giovanna Speranza, Niranjan Thatte </p>
<p>
The southern nucleus of NGC3256, a nearby luminous infrared (IR) interacting system (d=40 Mpc; L(IR)=10^11.6 Lsun), hosts a deeply obscured low-luminosity active galactic nucleus (AGN) associated with a massive and fast bipolar collimated molecular outflow. JWST/NIRSpec observations revealed bright spatially resolved ro-vibrational CO v=1-0 4.67um emission at the base of this molecular outflow extending out to ~300pc, which is excited by the ~4.7um IR radiation from the southern dusty core. Using Monte Carlo radiative transfer (MCRT) models, we analyzed the CO v=1-0 band emission to determine the intensity of the ~4.7um IR radiation field across the outflow base. We find that the core spectral luminosity density at ~4.7um, as seen by the material in the outflow base, is ~2-4 times higher than that inferred along our line of sight, indicating an anisotropic near-IR radiation field. Moreover, the slower than d^-2 decrease of the near-IR flux density across the outflow base suggests a collimated radiation field, for which we estimate a small half-opening angle (15-20deg) and a diameter of the collimating structure of 15 +- 5 pc. This is slightly smaller than the diameter of the obscuring envelope (25 +- 5 pc). Combined with the expansion of this obscuring envelope, traced by the blueshifted CO v=1-0 absorption toward the nucleus, this suggests that the AGN is beginning to clear its environment. We estimate N_H~10^22-23 cm<sup>2</sup> for NGC 3256 S, which is similar to that of Type 2 AGN. However, in contrast to Type 2 AGN, a high covering factor of the obscuring material, possibly sustained by inflows linked to the galaxy merger, and similar to that expected in deeply obscured compact obscured nuclei (CONs), is needed to explain the absence of extended high-ionization emission. Therefore, NGC 3256 S shares properties with CONs and Type 2 AGN.
</p>

### 27) [2611.07013](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07013v1): Latest νs from the Earliest Galaxies: Probing neutrino self-interactions with JWST

<p> Ranjini Mondol, Priyank Parashari, Anirban Das, Vera Gluscevic, Ranjan Laha </p>
<p>
Our Universe serves as a complementary laboratory for particle physics interactions. One intriguing possibility is that neutrinos may have beyond-the-Standard-Model (BSM) self-interactions, which can delay their free streaming and have an observable effect across different redshifts. Such interactions can induce scale-dependent changes in the matter power spectrum. The James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) has opened up a new window onto the early Universe by discovering a large population of high-redshift galaxies up to z ~ 15 and various probable galaxy candidates at even higher redshifts, between z ~ 15-30. Such high-redshift observations can probe the matter power spectrum on small scales, since structure formation is hierarchical and the structures hosting these high-redshift galaxies are therefore expected to be small. We, itfor the first time, use the UV luminosity function data from JWST and combine them with the itPlanck CMB data to probe neutrino self-interactions (νSI). If only one neutrino flavor experiences such BSM interactions, then itPlanck + JWST data give a 95% C.L. upper limit on Log_10(G_eff/MeV^-2) ≈ -2.75 and when all three flavors are interacting, the corresponding constraint becomes ≲ -3.41; both stronger than those obtained using only itPlanck data. Our results highlight the power of this new observable, the high-redshift UV luminosity function from JWST as a complementary probe to terrestrial experiments, showing that small-scale observables can open a new window onto smaller neutrino self-interaction strengths.
</p>

### 28) [2611.07006](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07006v2): Black Hole Seeds and Little Red Dots: Connecting Formation and Growth with a JWST Multi-Cycle Treasury Program

<p> Fabio Pacucci, Raffaella Schneider, Harley Katz, Brant E. Robertson, Rachel S. Somerville, Abdurro'uf, K. Aditya, Jose M. Afonso, Junya Arita, Guillermo Barro, Arghyadeep Basu, Ryan Begley, Sownak Bose, Marusa Bradac, Greg L. Bryan, Christopher Cain, Riccardo Caleno, Alex J. Cameron, Stefano Carniani, Hyunseop Choi, Nicholas Choustikov, Emma Curtis-Lake, Alessia D'Angelo, Romeel Dave, Rebecca L. Davies, Kelcey Davis, Callum T. Donnan, Qiao Duan, Yohan Dubois, Andreas L. Faisst, Marion Farcy, Steven L. Finkelstein, Steven Gillman, Simon C. O. Glover, Luca Graziani, Ansh R. Gupta, Thales A. Gutcke, Martin G. Haehnelt, Nimish P. Hathi, Evanthia Hatziminaoglou, Kasper E. Heintz, Michaela Hirschmann, Luis C. Ho, Lucy R. Ivey, Kartheik Iyer, Junehyoung Jeon, Brenda L. Jones, Ignas Juodzbalis, Anton M. Koekemoer, Christian Kragh Jespersen, Danial Langeroodi, Andrea Lapi, Muhammad A. Latif, Yuankang Liu, Christopher C. Lovell, Filippo Mannucci, Kentaro Nagamine, Priyamvada Natarajan, Zhiwei Pan, Robert Pascalau, Pablo G. Pérez-González, Elia Pizzati, Eniko Regos, Pierluigi Rinaldi, Daniel Roberts, William Roper, Dominik R. G. Schleicher, Francesco Shankar, Xuejian Shen, Thomas M. Stanton, Guochao Sun, Yang Sun, Alessandro Trinca, Jonathan R. Trump, Jack C. Turner, Hannah Übler, Rosa Valiante, Floor van Donkelaar, Giacomo Venturi, Aswin P. Vijayan, Fabio Vito, Tao Wang, Stephen Wilkins, John H. Wise, Joris Witstok, Zihao Wu, L. Y. Aaron Yung, Tommaso Zana, Huanian Zhang, Saiyang Zhang, Ming-Yang Zhuang </p>
<p>
JWST has revealed an abundant population of faint active galactic nuclei and a new class of compact, high-redshift sources known as Little Red Dots (LRDs), whose physical nature remains debated, with accreting massive black holes among the leading explanations. The emerging demographics of early black holes, with their high inferred number densities and, in some systems, the apparent excess of black hole mass relative to stellar mass, challenge simple scenarios of black hole seeding and growth. Despite these observational breakthroughs, the origin of early massive black holes remains uncertain, because "black holes have no memory": current samples cannot separate the imprint of seeding from subsequent growth. This white paper identifies the multi-cycle JWST observations needed to revolutionize our understanding of early black hole growth by: (i) distinguishing seeding pathways, (ii) constraining early black hole growth mechanisms, (iii) resolving the physical nature of broad-line emitters and LRDs, and (iv) testing black hole-galaxy co-evolution. These tasks require uniformly selected samples extending to low black hole and host-galaxy masses, allowing self-consistent measurements of black hole mass functions, Eddington-ratio distributions, and host properties. Grating spectroscopy, deep MIRI imaging, and variability analysis through repeated observations of the same fields would provide complementary tests of black hole formation and growth, their connection to host-galaxy evolution, and the nature of newly discovered populations of LRDs. In this white paper, we argue that these joint tests require a coordinated JWST Multi-Cycle Treasury program; such a program could provide the depth, uniformity, and time baseline needed to place substantially stronger, complementary constraints on early black hole formation, growth, and their connection to galaxy evolution.
</p>

### 29) [2611.06916](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06916v1): ReveaLLAGN 3: Revealing the Infrared Spectral Energy Distributions of Low Luminosity AGN

<p> David Ohlson, Anil C. Seth, Kameron Goold, Nischal Acharya, Torsten Böker, Antoine Dumont, Ruby Duncan, Michael Eracleous, Anja Feldmeier-Krause, Juan Antonio Fernández-Ontiveros, Elena Gallo, Andy D. Goulding, Jenny E. Greene, Luis C. Ho, Sera B. Markoff, Mallory Molina, Richard M. Plotkin, Almudena Prieto, Shobita Satyapal, Glenn van de Ven, Jonelle L. Walsh </p>
<p>
The spectral energy distributions of active galactic nuclei (AGN) provide valuable information on the emission mechanisms taking place in their inner regions. Here we use the James Webb Space Telescope to study the near and mid-infrared spectral energy distributions of seven nearby low-luminosity active galaxy nuclei from 1.7 to 24 μm. We cleanly separate the AGN spectrum by performing spatial fits of the two-dimensional images across all wavelengths. We then compare these clean unresolved spectra to aperture-based spectra and show that they effectively remove galaxy contamination. Comparing our infrared measurements to X-ray emission, we find that low-luminosity AGN have enhanced near-infrared emission relative to more luminous AGN, a trend that correlates with radio-loudness, while their mid-infrared-to-X-ray ratios are comparable. A majority of our targets show strong compact silicate emission suggesting the presence of warm dust on parsec scales in these systems. We also quantify the near- and mid-infrared power-law slopes and colors of low-luminosity AGN. In the near-infrared we find AGN of all luminosities have similar spectral slopes and colors, however in the mid-infrared, we find low-luminosity AGN typically have steeper slopes and redder colors than more luminous AGN. Both the strong silicate emission and steep mid-infrared slopes disagree with existing models for low-luminosity AGN spectral energy distributions.
</p>

### 30) [2611.06547](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06547v1): The dwarf galaxy NGC 185: the flagship of Andromeda's dwarf galaxies for the study of evolved multiple stellar populations

<p> Paolo Ventura, Chiara Ventura, Richard D'Souza, Flavia Dell'Agli, Matteo Correnti, Claudio Gavetti, Marco Tailo, Miriam Di Stefano, Eric Bell </p>
<p>
Reaching galaxies at 10-15 Mpc with HST and JWST requires star formation history (SFH) diagnostics that do not rely on the faint main sequence turn-off. We investigate whether horizontal branch (HB), red clump (RC), and red and asymptotic giant branch (RGB, AGB) stars can fill this role for dwarf elliptical galaxies, using NGC 185 as a test case. We use stellar evolution models of low- and intermediate-mass stars as inputs to population synthesis simulations that reproduce the colour distribution and luminosity function of stars in the (mF606W-mF814W, mF814W) diagram. The study of the stars populating the HB is used to derive the distance modulus and reddening, the average mass loss suffered by the stars during the ascending on the RGB and the SFH occurred during the ancient epochs, before 7 Gyr ago. The luminosity function of the RC gives us constraints on the recent SFH (&lt;8 Gyr). The distribution of HB stars yields a reddening E(B-V)=0.20 mag and a distance modulus DM=24.10 mag for NGC 185. The average mass lost by low-mass stars during the RGB phase is 0.22 +- 0.01 M<sub>&odot;</sub>. Star formation was concentrated at early epochs, with half of the stars formed more than 11 Gyr ago. The morphology of the HB indicates essentially no metallicity spread among stars older than 8 Gyr, whereas the slope of the RGB suggests that part of the stars younger than 5-6 Gyr are slightly enriched in metals. Once RGB mass loss is adequately constrained, evolved stars can provide a reliable SFH for dwarf elliptical galaxies well beyond the reach of MSTO-based methods. We discuss the implications for more distant systems and the remaining uncertainties in the underlying stellar models.
</p>

### 31) [2611.06443](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06443v1): AGN Host Galaxies in COSMOS-Web: Linking Structural Evolution and Star Formation Quenching up to z=5

<p> Xiao Han, Lilan Yang, T. Kelly, Matthew A. Malkan, Luis C. Ho, John D. Silverman, Zi-Jian Li, Andreas L. Faisst, Andrew J. Battisti, Jeyhan S. Kartaltepe, Lauro Moscardini, Rasha M. Samir, Ronaldo Laishram, Tao Wang, Yiyang Zhang, Caitlin M. Casey, Henry Joy McCracken, Jason Rhodes, Brant E. Robertson, Maximilien Franco, Daizhong Liu, Marko Shuntov, Xuheng Ding </p>
<p>
The role of active galactic nuclei (AGNs) in the suppression of star formation and structural evolution of galaxies during their transition from star-forming to quiescent systems remains unclear. We analyze 3,955 AGNs selected through five multiwavelength diagnostics at 0&lt;z&lt;5. Using high-resolution JWST imaging from the COSMOS-Web survey and archival multi-wavelength photometry, we investigate the rest-frame optical size-mass distribution of AGN host galaxies and compare it with those of star-forming galaxies (SFGs) and quiescent galaxies (QGs). We find that the positions of AGN hosts in the size-mass plane depend on both redshift and stellar mass. At z≳2, massive AGN hosts (M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>&gt;10^10.5) generally exhibit sizes comparable to those of SFGs despite suppressed star formation. Toward lower redshifts, they increasingly approach the QG size-mass relation, accompanied by declining specific star formation rates (sSFRs) and increasing Sérsic indices. In contrast, lower-mass AGN hosts remain close to the SFG relation across the redshift range. Overall, AGN hosts exhibit relatively mild size evolution, following R_e&prop;(1+z)^-0.6 over 0&lt;z&lt;4, shallower than that of typical SFGs and QGs. We further examine the relationship between star formation activity and structural compactness in the ΔlogSFR_MS-ΔΣ_e plane. At z&lt;2.2, AGN hosts predominantly occupy the compact star-forming and quiescent regions, broadly consistent with the compaction-quenching scenario. At z&gt;2.2, however, some AGN hosts exhibit suppressed star formation while retaining relatively extended structures. These findings suggest that some AGN hosts may follow alternative evolutionary pathways in which star formation suppression precedes substantial structural compaction.
</p>

### 32) [2611.06333](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06333v1): Understanding galaxies and the origin of the golden mass through the study of scaling relations across cosmic history

<p> C. Tortora, A. Gallazzi, A. Gargiulo, F. La Barbera, N. R. Napolitano, R. Ragusa, S. Zibetti </p>
<p>
The total mass distribution in galaxies strongly influences star formation (SF), both directly via gas availability and feedback, and indirectly via the dark matter (DM) potential well. Star formation efficiency peaks at a characteristic stellar mass of 5 × 10^10 M<sub>&odot;</sub>, often termed the "golden mass", where feedback mechanisms minimally affect SF. This scale also appears in other scaling relations: correlations between stellar population gradients, DM fraction, and total mass density slope versus stellar mass all show pronounced minima at the golden mass. How, why, and when does this golden mass emerge during galaxy evolution? Early cosmic epochs (z &gt; 1.5) remain largely unexplored, yet they probe the formative stages of galaxy mass assembly. They provide the full redshift baseline needed to constrain the buildup of stellar mass and the efficiency of gas-to-star conversion. By combining high resolution, multiplexing, and a wide field of view, SHARP on the ELT will address these questions, delivering sharper and deeper data than JWST. In this SHARP White Paper contribution, we present a scientific case exploiting these unique characteristics. SHARP will enable precise measurements of stellar population gradients, DM fractions, and total mass density slopes. Using the mIFU mode, SHARP will obtain spatially-resolved stellar population and dynamical maps for galaxies at z &gt; 1.5. Reconstructing these observables for early- and late-type systems will allow us to trace the emergence of the golden mass across cosmic time, and to decode the impact of galaxy processes (mergers, SN, and AGN feedback) by comparing observations with cosmological simulations. (abridged)
</p>

### 33) [2611.06026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06026v1): Spectroscopy-informed Deep Learning Searches for Photometric Little Red Dots in JWST Surveys

<p> Chenxuan Zhang, Huanian Zhang, Qingwen Wu </p>
<p>
Little Red Dots (LRDs) are a population of compact, red sources discovered at high redshift with JWST that exhibit distinctive V-shaped spectral energy distributions (SEDs). Existing LRD samples are typically identified using a variety of color-based selection criteria, which can lead to differences in the populations recovered by different studies. We develop a cross-modal machine-learning framework that combines matched NIRCam imaging and NIRSpec/PRISM spectroscopy to transfer spectroscopic information into imaging-based representations, enabling LRD identification from JWST/NIRCam imaging alone. Applied to spectroscopically observed high-redshift galaxies previously classified as non-LRDs, our model identifies 35 new LRD candidates. The majority satisfy at least one independent LRD color selection criterion, and among the 13 candidates with medium- or high-resolution NIRSpec grating spectra, 10 exhibit broad Hα emission - a hallmark of LRDs. We further apply the classifier to public NIRCam imaging from the Dawn JWST Archive and two independent JWST surveys, yielding 357 and 23 additional LRD candidates, respectively. The candidates recovered by our method span a broader range of observed SED properties than those in existing samples, suggesting that current color-selected samples may not fully capture the diversity of LRD-like systems present in JWST surveys. Our findings highlight the value of spectroscopy-informed photometric classification as a powerful strategy for systematically mining large-area JWST imaging datasets and provide a practical framework for future studies of this emerging population.
</p>

### 34) [2611.05496](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05496v1): ReveaLLAGN 2: Exploring the Nuclear Gas and Stellar Kinematics of Nearby LLAGNs with JWST/NIRSpec IFU

<p> Antoine Dumont, Nadine Neumayer, Anil C. Seth, Torsten Böker, Jonelle L. Walsh, Micheal Eracleous, Luis C. Ho, Kameron Goold, David Ohlson, Timothy A. Davis </p>
<p>
Low-luminosity active galactic nuclei (LLAGN) represent the most common mode of black hole accretion in the local Universe, yet their nuclear structure, stellar and gas kinematics remain poorly constrained at parsec scales. We investigate the nuclear regions of eight nearby representative LLAGN, the ReveaLLAGN sample: M87, M104, M94, M81, NGC1052, NGC4258, NGC5128 and NGC4395. Using JWST/NIRSpec data, we probe the stellar, molecular hydrogen, hydrogen recombination, and high ionisation coronal lines kinematics and their spatial distribution, exploring how detectability and spatial distributions of these lines vary across different Eddington ratios. The stellar LOSV maps reveal a slowly rotating disk in the nuclei of all our targets, characterised by an anti-correlation with the h3 Gauss-Hermite moment. Across the sample, the stellar velocity dispersion rises toward the central black hole. Only in M104 and M94 does the velocity dispersion display a more intricate pattern that departs from a simple disk configuration, indicating the presence of multiple kinematic components in these galaxies. Across the sample, the gas distribution and kinematics exhibit rotation that is frequently decoupled from the stellar rotation, suggesting that the stellar gravitational potential is decoupled from the current accretion state. The gas kinematics are often visually aligned with the radio jet, presenting broader velocity dispersions along the jet direction. Line-ratio maps indicate that the dominant excitation mechanism is photoionisation by the central AGN, with mechanical heating from shocks becoming important only at higher Eddington ratios. Across the sample, we detect coronal lines in all LLAGN when using nuclear-aperture spectra, and we can obtain spatially resolved kinematics only in NGC5128 and NGC4395, the highest Eddington ratio sources.
</p>

### 35) [2611.05486](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05486v1): The M87 Black Hole Mass from JWST and Keck Stellar Kinematics Spanning 10 pc to 10 kpc

<p> Emily R. Liepold, Chung-Pei Ma, Refa M. Al-Amri, Jonelle L. Walsh </p>
<p>
We combine JWST NIRSpec and Keck KCWI observations to construct a contiguous 2D map of M87's stellar kinematics spanning three decades in projected radius, from ~10 pc to ~10 kpc, and measure the mass of its central supermassive black hole. The innermost stellar kinematic measurements probe projected radii as small as ~3% of the black hole's sphere of influence radius, or approximately 30,000 Schwarzschild radii. The NIRSpec spectra within R &lt; 0.45'' are contaminated by quasiperiodic "wiggle" artifacts. We develop hyphen, which uses detector spectral traces to predict how the wiggle frequency varies with wavelength, then fits and subtracts the wiggle pattern from each spectrum in each exposure before combining the data. The cleaned spectra reveal a pronounced central rise in stellar velocity dispersion and h_4. Triaxial Schwarzschild stellar orbit modeling with the TriOS code, fitting ~5,000 kinematic constraints across 618 spatial bins from JWST and Keck, yields a black hole mass of M_BH=(5.38 ± 0.13± 0.22)×10^9 M<sub>&odot;</sub>. This result is consistent with our prior KCWI-only determination but with the statistical uncertainty reduced by a factor of 2-3, now subdominant to the systematic uncertainty in the distance (D) to M87. Our inferred mass-to-distance ratio is θ_g=GM_BH/(c^2 D) = 3.16 ± 0.08  μas, whose 90% credible interval overlaps with that obtained from the multi-epoch Event Horizon Telescope (EHT) analysis, providing a robust and independent prior for future EHT studies.
</p>

### 36) [2611.05487](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05487v1): A pocket of oxygen in a near-pristine galaxy at z=4.2: chemical inhomogeneity at 10-parsec scales in LAP2

<p> M. Messa, E. Vanzella, S. Rojas-Ruiz, T. Treu, P. Bergamini, G. Di Rosa, M. Lombardi, P. Rosati, A. Zanella, A. Adamo, A. Bolamperti, F. Calura, M. Castellano, R. Gilli, C. Grillo, U. Meštrić </p>
<p>
We aim to constrain the oxygen abundance, the ionizing properties, and the spatial structure of LAP2, a strongly lensed (with a magnification μ~50) oxygen-deficient galaxy at z=4.2 behind the galaxy cluster Abell 2744. We present new JWST/NIRSpec IFU PRISM observations (~13.6 hr) of LAP2, which we combine with archival JWST/NIRCam imaging. LAP2 is intrinsically extremely faint (M_UV=-12.7) and has a low stellar mass (M~10^5~M<sub>&odot;</sub>), comparable to that of massive star clusters. It consists of a compact core (R_eff~7 pc) embedded in a more diffuse emission (with R_eff~20 pc). Strong Hα emission (EW_rest≃880 Å, logξ<sub>ion</sub>≃25.3), a blue UV slope (β_UV≃-2.5, down to ≃-3.1 in the core), and a Balmer decrement consistent with no dust attenuation indicate a young (≲10 Myr), dust-free burst. [OIII]λ5007 is not detected in the integrated spectrum, implying [OIII]/Hβ≤0.61 (3σ) and, using an extrapolated strong-line calibration, 12+log(O/H)≤6.4 (≲0.5&percnt; Z<sub>&odot;</sub>). This places LAP2 among the most metal-poor galaxies known. Faint [OIII] emission is, however, detected in the core (with 4σ significance), where we derive 12+log(O/H)≃6.6 (~1&percnt; Z<sub>&odot;</sub>). This difference can be explained either by local self-enrichment, requiring only ~0.2 M<sub>&odot;</sub> of newly synthesized oxygen, or by a higher ionization parameter in the core. The upper limit on HeIIλ1640 (EW_rest&lt;39 Å, 3σ) does not exclude a contribution from metal-free stars. LAP2 extends the census of spectroscopically confirmed EMPGs to star-cluster masses and to M_UV≈-13. Its location in a low-density region supports a scenario in which underdense environments preserve nearly pristine gas well after reionization.
</p>

### 37) [2611.05349](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05349v1): UV spectra of little red dots: Broad-line N/C, gas recycling and black hole masses

<p> Albert Sneppen, Darach Watson, Lucy R. Ivey, Kasper E. Heintz, Alex J. Cameron, Joris Witstok, Vadim Rusakov, James H. Matthews, Rasmus Damgaard, Georgios Nikopoulos, Vasily Belokurov </p>
<p>
Radiative-transfer models of supermassive black holes accreting inside dense gas cocoons reproduce the X-ray-to-near-infrared spectral energy distribution of "little red dots" (LRDs) using the code Sirocco. In the ultraviolet, these models also predict weak broad emission lines and a series of metal absorption lines, which deep spectroscopy from the JWST SPURS survey now detects in several LRDs. Here we use the models to interpret these features. We find that N and C ions of equal charge (3+ or 2+) occupy largely the same gas in the simulations, such that the combination of the broad N IV]/C IV and N III]/C III] flux ratios simultaneously constrains both the electron density and the N/C abundance ratio of the gas in the LRD proper. The nitrogen and carbon emission of SPURS-GN-2, an LRD at z=7.19, requires an order of magnitude nitrogen excess compared to the solar value, N/C = 12^+3_-2 (N/C)<sub>&odot;</sub>, in the broad-line gas with electron density log n_e = 9.7^+0.3_-0.5 cm^-3. Thus, around half the broad-line CNO nuclei have passed through hydrogen burning. Because the ionised gas is replaced on a century timescale, sustaining this enrichment favours gas recycling. UV absorption lines test this independently. In two of the four LRDs, line blueshift velocities clearly increase with the photon energy required to ionise the absorber gas. In the models the absorption lines are spatially stratified, indicating that the gas is launched near the escape speed and slowed by gravity. In GN-2, where the measurement can be made, the inferred deceleration requires radiation pressure close to the Eddington limit of a 10^6-7 M<sub>&odot;</sub> black hole. The lines are also typically strongly saturated, making inferences of direct abundance ratios misleading.
</p>

### 38) [2611.04605](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04605v1): Probing the Faint Outskirts of Star-Forming Galaxies with 1&lt;z&lt;9 Using Stacked Images

<p> Cheng Jia, Enci Wang, Yangyao Chen, Cheqiu Lyu, Hongxin Zhang, Ziwen Zhang, Jinyang Wang, Jie Song, Bojun Tao, Xiaoxuan Chen, Junsheng Li </p>
<p>
We present a systematic study of the faint outer light profiles of star-forming galaxies using deep JWST/NIRCam imaging from the CEERS, JADES, and PRIMER surveys. Our sample consists of 14,812 face-on disk galaxies at 1&lt;z&lt;9 with log(M_*/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)&gt;8.5, selected from the DAWN JWST Archive. To characterize the low-surface-brightness outskirts beyond the effective radius (R_e), we stack galaxies in bins of stellar mass and redshift, accounting for cosmological surface-brightness dimming, angular-size scaling, and PSF convolution, and estimate residual backgrounds from blank-sky stacks. The color profiles generally become bluer toward larger radii, indicating negative color gradients and supporting an inside-out growth scenario. Galaxies with log(M_*/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) &lt; 9.5 at z&gt;2.5 exhibit a weak reversal in their outermost regions, suggesting more complex stellar population gradients at early times. The outer light profiles generally exhibit a good exponential decline with radius, and the outer scale lengths measured beyond 2R_e show mass and redshift dependences remarkably similar to those of the conventional mass-size relation. This similarity suggests that the extended outer light is closely linked to the same processes governing the growth of the inner galaxy, rather than providing a direct tracer of ex-situ stellar components in the early universe. We also find little wavelength dependence in the outer scale lengths, indicating that dust attenuation likely has a limited impact at these radii. These results provide new constraints on the buildup of extended stellar distributions during cosmic noon and at earlier epochs.
</p>
