### 80) [2610.33161](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33161v1): SN Ares: A Strongly Lensed Type IIb Supernova at z=1.276 with a Predicted 70-Year Time Delay

<p> Conor Larison, Justin D. R. Pierel, Matthew Siebert, Tom Resseguier, Lukas J. Furtak, Masamune Oguri, Gavin S. Farley, Hollis B. Akins, Yoshihisa Asada, Rachel Bezanson, Maruša Bradač, Gabriel Brammer, John Chisholm, Dan Coe, Pratika Dayal, Henry C. Ferguson, Steven L. Finkelstein, Seiji Fujimoto, Tomokazu Kiyota, Anton M. Koekemoer, Vasily Kokorev, Minami Nakane, Masami Ouchi, Richard Pan, Casey Papovich, Johan Richard, Luke Robbins, Fengwu Sun, Kana Takechi, Tommaso Treu, Chris Willott, Hiroto Yanagisawa, Abdurro'uf, Joseph F. V. Allingham, Ricardo O. Amorín, Franz E. Bauer, Larry Bradley, Mateusz Bronikowski, Christopher J. Conselice, David A. Coulter, James M. DerKacy, José M. Diego, Eiichi Egami, Andreas L. Faisst, Ori D. Fox, Brenda L. Frye, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Mauro González-Otero, Massimo Griggio, Nimish P. Hathi, Tiger Yu-Yang Hsiao, Saurabh W. Jha, Y. Jiménez-Teja, Jeyhan S. Kartaltepe, Gourav Khullar, Zachary G. Lane, Paulo A. A. Lopes, Ray A. Lucas, Georgios E. Magdis, Matteo Messa, Rohan P. Naidu, Rafael Ortiz, Massimo Pascale, Armin Rest, Massimo Ricotti, Melissa Shahbandeh, William Sheu, Koji Shukawa, Rogier A. Windhorst, Anita Zanella, Yossef Zenati, Adi Zitrin </p>
<p>
We present the discovery of supernova (SN) Ares, a strongly lensed supernova at z<sub>spec</sub>=1.276±0.002 in a massive, triply imaged host galaxy behind the MACS J0308.9+2645 galaxy cluster. JWST/NIRSpec spectroscopy spanning the rest-frame optical to NIR of Ares reveals helium features at 5876 \mathringA, 7065 \mathringA, and 10830 \mathringA, as well as Hα and Paβ. A comparison to local core-collapse SN spectra and light curves leads to a classification of SN Ares as a Type IIb supernova, the highest-redshift member of its class spectroscopically identified. A comparison to local SN IIb light curves provides a phase estimate for the spectrum of 34_-9^+10 rest-frame days post-radioactive peak, providing a unique view into a high-redshift stripped-envelope supernova in its late-photospheric evolution. A lens model of the cluster constructed using recent JWST data indicates that SN Ares is the first image to arrive and will reappear in two future images near the center of the cluster plane in ≳ 70 years, with predicted magnifications of ~5× and ~1.5×. Planned JWST/NIRSpec Multi-Object Spectroscopy of the cluster will allow for more detailed modeling of the lensed system for tighter constraints on the time delay and magnifications, as well as a template spectrum at the location of SN Ares.
</p>

### 81) [2610.33154](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33154v1): A Composite Rest-Frame UV Spectrum of Galaxies at 5.7 &lt; z &lt; 6.9: Ionization, Outflows, and Implications for Intergalactic Metal Enrichment

<p> David DePalma, Robert A. Simcoe, Anna-Christina Eilers, Rongmon Bordoloi, Daichi Kashino, Gauri Kotiwale, Jorryt Matthee </p>
<p>
We present a stacked JWST/NIRSpec spectrum of 104 galaxies at λ_rest=1200&ndash;2000\mathringA to study nebular emission and absorption from the interstellar medium (ISM) and stellar winds. The sample is drawn from known [O III] emitters with mean redshift z=6.50, absolute UV magnitude M_UV=-20.7, and stellar mass M<sub>&sext;</sub>=10^8.93M<sub>&odot;</sub>. The stacked continuum closely resembles lower-redshift Lyman Break Galaxy composites, with a few notable differences. Lyα has comparable strength and is redshifted relative to the [O III] systemic frame, consistent with production in gaseous outflows. High-ionization emission lines including O III], C III], and He II are slightly stronger than in lower-redshift stacks but exhibit narrow widths, disfavoring ionization from a broad-line AGN. Emission-line ratios slightly favor ionization by a hard stellar continuum, though a contribution from a narrow-line nuclear source cannot be strictly excluded. Numerous strong absorption lines at both high and low ionization are blueshifted by ~200km s<sup>-1</sup>, again indicating outflows driven by stellar winds or entrained metal-enriched ISM. A simple model suggests that at z~6.5 most star-forming galaxies are too young for winds at these velocities to propagate beyond ~3&ndash;9 proper kpc, implying that the observed [O III] emitter population alone cannot explain the frequency of heavy-element absorbers seen in quasar spectra. In this picture, early enrichment of the intergalactic medium likely arises from undetected low-mass galaxies (M<sub>&sext;</sub>~10^5&ndash;10^6M<sub>&odot;</sub>) clustered around the more massive systems detected in JWST/NIRCam WFSS surveys.
</p>

### 82) [2610.32497](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32497v1): Star clusters at cosmic dawn I: the emergence of the initial cluster mass function

<p> Fred S. Thompson, Julien Devriendt, Adrianne Slyz, Taysun Kimm, Cheonsu Kang </p>
<p>
JWST observations of lensed galaxies reveal unusually bright star-cluster candidates within the first billion years after the Big Bang. However, the physical processes shaping the initial cluster mass function (ICMF) remain uncertain. We study the formation of a galaxy embedded in a dark matter halo whose virial mass reaches 10^10 M<sub>&odot;</sub> at z=6, using cosmological radiation-hydrodynamics zoom-in simulations. Its star clusters, represented by sink particles, accrete gas and form stellar populations that supply radiation and supernova feedback, thereby regulating their own growth. As we do not prescribe an efficiency per free-fall time or a fixed star-cluster formation duration, the ICMF emerges self-consistently. We assess the effects of numerical resolution and stellar radiation. Schechter fits to the central-galaxy ICMF give truncation masses near 6 × 10^6 M<sub>&odot;</sub>, with substantial uncertainty. At fixed resolution, including stellar radiation makes the low-mass slope of the ICMF shallower, from -1.60 to -1.34, while doubling the spatial resolution steepens it back to -1.54. Stellar radiation also reduces the abundance of low-mass clouds, particularly those with lower gas densities, and shortens the formation time of star clusters to about a Myr, i.e. well before their first supernovae go off. On average, more rapidly fed star clusters accrete for longer, making their final masses rise faster than linearly with the initial accretion rate and contributing to a flatter ICMF. At fixed host-galaxy star formation rate, our brightest simulated clusters reach UV luminosities comparable to those of observed lensed star-cluster candidates.
</p>

### 83) [2610.32430](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32430v1): Local Analogs of Little Red Dots: Clues to the X-ray Weakness

<p> Shi-Jiang Chen, Jun-Xian Wang, Zhen-Ya Zheng, Ruqiu Lin, Luis C. Ho, Zhen-Yi Cai, Chang-Hao Chen, Zsofi Igo, Jia-Lai Kang, Ruancun Li, Teng Liu, Wenke Ren, Zhen-Bo Su </p>
<p>
A major question about JWST's little red dots (LRDs) at z~4-8 is the origin of their apparent X-ray weakness. Even deep Chandra stacks yield only tentative detections or broad-band limits and cannot distinguish among super-Eddington accretion, intrinsic X-ray weakness, and Compton-thick (CT) obscuration. Local analogs offer a powerful complementary route. At z~0.3, similarly luminous sources require ~10^3-4 less exposure for comparable signal-to-noise, suffer less source confusion, and directly probe the diagnostic rest-frame soft X-rays. Here we present a pilot Einstein Probe/FXT study of six "local little red dots" (LLRDs), V-shaped broad-line green pea galaxies selected as low-redshift counterparts of LRDs. These sources show X-ray diversity: "X-normal" LLRDs resemble type 1 AGNs in X-ray luminosity and optical variability, whereas the "X-weak" stack lies ~0.5-0.7 dex below standard unobscured-quasar scaling relations while remaining compatible with the representative LRD stack and 95% of published individual limits. The X-weak LLRDs also lack detected optical variability and have redder optical continua than X-normal LLRDs, making their V-shaped SEDs closer to those of high-redshift LRDs. The detection of the X-weak stack at ~3.3σ with the soft-X-ray-sensitive EP-FXT provides rest-frame ~2-3 keV constraints difficult to reconcile with a typical unobscured super-Eddington model or a local-AGN-like CT geometry examined here, although modified variants remain possible. The simplest alternative is a mildly X-ray-weak corona (Δα_ox~-0.1 to -0.2) with Compton-thin absorption (N_H~10^22-23 cm<sup>2</sup>), consistent with the independent Balmer decrement/break diagnostics. Together with growing samples of local LRD analogs, our results offer a practical path for testing the physical nature of LRD X-ray weakness.
</p>

### 84) [2610.32307](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32307v1): Galaxy Luminosity Functions at z~7-10 from Four Years of JWST Observations

<p> Bangzheng Sun, Haojing Yan </p>
<p>
We present galaxy luminosity functions at z≈ 7.4 and 10 based on a comprehensive search for candidate galaxies at 6.4≲ z≲11.3 in JWST's public NIRCam data four years after its operation. Our work utilises nine fields covering ~1,045~arcmin<sup>2</sup> in total, including seven blank fields and two galaxy cluster fields. Using the dropout technique, we selected 3,733 dropouts from the F090W passband and 2,396 dropouts from the F115W passband, which constitute the largest samples of candidate galaxies at z≈ 7.4 and z≈ 10 to date. Among these objects, 338 F090W-dropouts and 99 F115W-dropouts already have secure redshifts from JWST NIRSpec spectroscopy over four years, which we use to form two large spectroscopic subsamples to assess the magnitude-dependent contamination rates of their parent dropout samples. Our luminosity functions are based on the results after statistically correcting for both incompleteness and contamination. We find that the luminosity function at z≈ 7.4 can be well described by a conventional Schechter function but with a faint-end slope of α=-1.70±0.16 that is significantly shallower than most of the previous studies at z≈ 7&ndash;8. To verify this result, more imaging and spectroscopy at ~30~mag will be needed. The luminosity function at z≈ 10, on the other hand, can hardly be fitted by a Schechter function but is more consistent with a power law in the form of Φ(M)&prop; 10^-0.4(β+1)M, with the best-fit slope of β=-2.36±0.16. While such a behavior is largely driven by the high number densities at M_UV≲ -21.8~mag, it is supported by the currently existing spectroscopic confirmations. Further spectroscopy at the bright end(≲ 26~mag) will be needed to strengthen this result.
</p>

### 85) [2610.31078](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31078v1): Self-Supervised Representation Learning for JWST Galaxy Images and Low Resolution Spectra

<p> Yang Cheng, Morgan Fouesneau, Ivelina G. Momcheva, Tobias Buck, Shengxiu Sun </p>
<p>
A self-supervised multimodal model recovers galaxy stellar masses from sparse JWST data that lack the multi-band coverage required by traditional SED fitting. We train on single-band F150W imaging paired with observed-frame 1-2 μm PRISM spectra and obtain a joint image-spectrum representation that recovers stellar mass at 1&lt;z≤3 with R^2 ~ 0.9 and σ_NMAD = 0.24 dex; cross-modal CLIP alignment further raises single-modality performance by ΔR^2 = +0.04 (imaging) and +0.09 (spectroscopy), narrowing the gap to joint-modality inference. The code and documentation are available at \hrefhttps://github.com/2003cy/neurips2026_ssl_jwst GitHub.

### 86) [2610.30604](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30604v2): Multiwavelength Classification of Chandra Sources in Omega Centauri

<p> Steven Chen, Oleg Kargaltsev, Jeremy Hare, Hui Yang, Oleg Smirnov, Ian Heywood, Talon Myburgh </p>
<p>
We present a comprehensive study of X-ray sources in the globular cluster NGC 5139 to date. Over 200 Chandra sources are cross-matched to multiwavelength catalogs from HST, JWST, and MeerKAT. We employ a machine-learning classification pipeline (MUWCLASS) for the first time in such a crowded stellar environment and introduce the globular-cluster-specific training dataset, which compiles previously classified sources from 25 Galactic globular clusters. Given the challenges presented by the high-density environment, we compare the MUWCLASS results with traditional classification methods. We confirm counterpart associations suggested in previous works and also identify new confident and candidate associations. We discuss the implications of our classification results in light of models of cluster dynamical evolution and binary formation/destruction.

### 87) [2610.30441](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30441v1): JWST observations of the planetary nebula NGC 6302 IV: PAH spectral characteristics, formation and evolution

<p> Nicholas Clark, Els Peeters, Jan Cami, Mikako Matsuura, Alexander G. G. M. Tielens, Patrick J. Kavanagh, Michael J. Barlow, Joel Kastner, Kathleen E. Kraemer, Hektor Monteiro, Raghvendra Sahai, G. C. Sloan, N. C. Sterling, Jeremy R. Walsh, L. B. F. M. Waters, Nicholas J. Wright, Isabel Aleman, Bruce Balick, Jeronimo Bernard-Salas, Charmi Bhatt, Joris Blommaert, Harriet L. Dinerstein, Naomi Hirano, Olivia Jones, Francisca Kemper, Eric Lagadec, Paula Moraga Baez, Maryam Torki, Peter A. M. van Hoof, Kevin Volk, Roger Wesson, Albert Zijlstra </p>
<p>
Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) and carbonaceous dust have been observed in Oxygen-rich planetary nebulae (PNe), yet their formation and evolution in these environments is an open question. We aim to study the formation and evolution of the PAHs in the O-rich PN NGC 6302, by characterizing the PAH emission throughout this object. We obtained JWST MIRI IFU spectroscopic observations of NGC 6302 pointed at its central source. From these, we extract four template spectra that are representative of the variation in PAH emission throughout NGC 6302. We detect the major PAH features at 6.2, 7.7, 8.6, 11.2, and 12.7 μm, and several minor PAH features. The template spectra vary in terms of their PAH feature widths, peak wavelengths, and relative intensities. The 11.2 μm PAH feature is class B, typical for PNe. The 6-9 μm PAH features are class A, which is less common for PNe. We estimate the PAH charge to be 75% cations on average, based on the 7.7/11.2 PAH feature ratio. The relative strengths of the 11-15 μm PAH features indicate a range of PAH edge structures. To ensure consistency in our observations and explain variable PAH feature widths, we propose a variation in the size of the PAHs throughout NGC 6302, and suggest variation in the shielding of these PAHs. The PAHs range from large and non-compact, to small and compact. We find that NGC 6302 formed PAHs in-situ in the current PN phase, likely from small hydrocarbons formed by the photodissociation of CO.
</p>

### 88) [2610.29887](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29887v2): A Little Red Dot emerging from a nitrogen-rich UV-luminous galaxy with Very Massive Stars

<p> Alberto Torralba, Jorryt Matthee, Rui Marques-Chaves, Fabrice Martins, Benjamín Navarrete, Gabriele Pezzulli, Edoardo Iani, Rohan P. Naidu, Gabriel Brammer, Claudia Di Cesare, Anne Hutter, Gauri Kotiwale, Ruari Mackenzie, Sara Mascia, Romain Meyer, Daniel Schaerer </p>
<p>
JWST is revealing abundant populations of high-z galaxies with unforeseen properties: UV-luminous galaxies with high nitrogen enrichment, populations of very massive stars (VMSs; &gt;100 M<sub>&odot;</sub>), and early forms of massive black hole growth such as the Little Red Dots (LRDs). Here we present all these phenomena appearing simultaneously in COLA1, a luminous (M_UV=-21.3) galaxy at z=6.6. New NIRSpec medium-resolution data reveal a blue UV continuum (β_UV=-2.5) with strong emission lines including NIV] &lambda;1486, stellar P Cygni profiles, and broad HeII &lambda;1640. We fit the UV with stellar population models, and find that the models including VMSs (limited to 300 M<sub>&odot;</sub>) are statistically preferred. The best-fit stellar population has M_*≈10^8 M<sub>&odot;</sub>, and a young age of 2.4±0.8 Myr. The rest-optical shows a redder continuum (β_opt=-1.1) and broad Balmer lines with Balmer absorption akin to LRD spectra. By evolving the best-fit stellar population we find that the UV could dim by 1-2 mag in ~5 Myr, and hence this system would be observed as a typical V-shaped LRD. We measure a supersolar log_10(N/O)=-0.07±0.05 and subsolar log_10(C/O)=-0.64±0.11; therefore log_10(N/C)=+0.58±0.12. The independent density-sensitive NIV] λ&lambda;1483,1486 and CIII] λ&lambda;1906/1909 ratios require two zones with n_e≳10^6.5 cm^-3 and n_e≲10^3.5 cm^-3, respectively. We estimate log_10(N/C)&gt;1.3 (2σ) for the dense zone, requiring extreme N/C enrichment mechanisms such as highly rotating &gt;100 M<sub>&odot;</sub> VMSs, tidal disruption events, or supermassive stars. We speculate that runaway collisions of massive stars in a dense stellar cluster may have lead to the seeding of an intermediate-mass black hole resulting in the formation of an LRD.
</p>

### 89) [2610.29405](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29405v1): FEAST: Spatially Resolved Star Formation History of the NGC 4485/NGC 4490 System and its Tidal Bridge from JWST/NIRCam Color Magnitude Diagrams

<p> Giacomo Bortolini, Matteo Correnti, Angela Adamo, Michele Cignoni, Monica Tosi, Göran Östlin, Arjan Bik, Daniela Calzetti, John M. Cannon, Flavia Dell'Agli, Bruce G. Elmegreen, Helena Faustino Vieira, Kathryn Grasha, Kelsey E. Johnson, Thomas S. -Y. Lai, Drew Lapeer, Sean T. Linden, Matteo Messa, Alex Pedrini, Julia Roman-Duval, Elena Sabbi, Linda J. Smith </p>
<p>
We present the spatially resolved star formation history (SFH) of the interacting, low-mass, gas-rich galaxy pair NGC 4485/NGC 4490, based on deep JWST/NIRCam imaging obtained as part of the FEAST program. Using resolved stars' near-infrared color-magnitude diagrams (CMDs) and the SFERA 2.0 CMD fitting code, we reconstruct the SFH of the system, including the tidal bridge connecting the two galaxies. We find that recent star formation is widespread across the system, extending beyond the main bodies of the galaxies into the tidal bridge and other off-center areas. In particular, a significant fraction of the recent star formation in NGC 4485 occurs outside its central region. All major regions of the system exhibit two prominent episodes of enhanced star formation, occurring around 0.5-1 Gyr and 10-30 Myr ago, with the tidal bridge showing the largest increase relative to its average star formation rate. These peaks in star formation activity occur a few hundred million years after the epochs of the first and second pericenter passages predicted by independent dynamical models, supporting a scenario in which repeated tidal interactions triggered the observed bursts through gas redistribution and compression. Our results place direct observational constraints on the formation history of NGC 4485/NGC 4490 and provide evidence for the important role of galaxy interactions in driving spatially extended, bursty star formation in low-mass galaxies.
</p>

### 90) [2610.29285](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29285v1): B-fields In STar-forming Region Observations (BISTRO): Dual-Wavelength Constraints on Size-Dependent Grain Alignment in Star-Forming Regions

<p> Ray S. Furuya, David Berry, Pierre Bastien, Tetsuo Hasegawa, Masafumi Matsumura, Thiem Hoang, Lapo Fanciullo, Kazunari Iwasaki, Simon Coudé, Takashi Onaka, Pham Ngoc Diep, Sarah Sadavoy, Hua-bai Li, Koji Kawabata, Doris Arzoumanian, Yasuo Doi, Jia-Wei Wang, Tao-Chung Ching, Janik Karoly, Jihye Hwang, Glenn J. White, A-Ran Lyo, Kohji Tomisaka, Le Ngoc Tram, Frédérick Poidevin, Valentin J. M. Le Gouellec, Hiroko Shinnaga, Masato I. N. Kobayashi, Junhao Liu, Mehrnoosh Tahani, Nguyen Bich Ngoc, Jennifer Hatchell, Archana Soam, Hsi-Wei Yen, Chakali Eswaraiah, Vera Könyves, Jintai Wu, Jeong-Eun Lee, Yusuke Tsukamoto, Ekta Sharma, Tie Liu, Qilao Gu, Hiroyuki Nakanishi, Tsuyoshi Inoue, Fumitaka Nakamura, Rachel Friesen, Jean-François Robitaille, Yoshiaki Misugi, Raiga Kashiwagi, Per Friberg, Sarah Graves, Mark Rawlings, Harriet Parsons, David Eden, Huei-Ru Vivien Chen, Najimudeen Bijas, Motohide Tamura, Tyler L. Bourke, Nagayoshi Ohashi, Woojin Kwon, Jongsoo Kim, Shih-Ping Lai, Keping Qiu, Di Li, Shu-ichiro Inutsuka, Kate Pattle, Derek Ward-Thompson, BISTRO Collaboration </p>
<p>
We compared 450 and 850 μm POL-2 observations from the James Clerk Maxwell Telescope at 12.6'' resolution. The median absolute difference in polarization angle between bands is 6.7&deg; across 21 regions. For total intensity I&prop;ν^α and polarized intensity PI&prop;ν^γ, 24-27 of 35 regions have γ&lt;α, allowing for 3σ limits on weak polarized intensities. The median γ-α is -0.51, ranging from -0.57 to -0.45 under these limits. The ratio of polarization fractions is P_450/P_850=0.69±0.02_sys±0.03_stat, with no detected dependence on the angle difference. We suggest that the dependence of alignment on grain size offers the simplest explanation of these findings. For optically thin components sharing a polarization angle and opacity law, temperature mixing reproduces the median -0.51 only if the warm dust is polarized less efficiently than the cold dust. If the cold dust is at 10 K or warmer and the warm dust at 40 K or cooler, the median requires a ratio of warm to cold effective polarization efficiencies r≲0.18. We tested mixtures with opacity index β=2 in four fields against Herschel dust temperatures and Green Bank Ammonia Survey kinetic temperatures. We assumed equal gas and dust temperatures, ammonia tracing cold gas, and model ranges of 3-20 K for cold dust and 25-40 K for warm dust carrying at most half the mass. Mixtures matching both temperatures cannot reproduce γ-α within 1σ in three fields, including OMC 2 tentatively, even for r=0. Allowing warm dust at 10-100 K and up to 95% of the mass permits solutions only for r≤0.041. To explain these three fields by temperature mixing, we need wider model ranges and a very strong contrast in polarization efficiency. The origin of this contrast remains undetermined.
</p>

### 91) [2610.29273](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29273v1): B-fields In STar-forming Region Observations (BISTRO): Technical Foundations for Quantitative 450/850 μm JCMT SCUBA-2/POL-2 Polarimetry

<p> Ray S. Furuya, David Berry, Pierre Bastien, Tetsuo Hasegawa, Masafumi Matsumura, Koji Kawabata, Kazunari Iwasaki, Per Friberg, Simon Coudé, Lapo Fanciullo, Thiem Hoang, Yasuo Doi, Hua-bai Li, Jia-Wei Wang, Tao-Chung Ching, Hiroko Shinnaga, Hiroyuki Nakanishi, Sarah Graves, A-Ran Lyo, Junhao Liu, Janik Karoly, Jihye Hwang, Glenn J. White, Doris Arzoumanian, Sarah Sadavoy, Kohji Tomisaka, Pham Ngoc Diep, Mehrnoosh Tahani, Nguyen Bich Ngoc, Archana Soam, Hsi-Wei Yen, Chakali Eswaraiah, Vera Könyves, Jeong-Eun Lee, Valentin J. M. Le Gouellec, Ekta Sharma, Le Ngoc Tram, Frédérick Poidevin, Tie Liu, Mark Rawlings, Najimudeen Bijas, Fumitaka Nakamura, Rachel Friesen, Jean-François Robitaille, Yoshiaki Misugi, Raiga Kashiwagi, Takashi Onaka, Motohide Tamura, Harriet Parsons, Tyler L. Bourke, David Eden, Huei-Ru Vivien Chen, Nagayoshi Ohashi, Jongsoo Kim, Shih-Ping Lai, Keping Qiu, Di Li, Woojin Kwon, Shu-ichiro Inutsuka, Kate Pattle, Derek Ward-Thompson, BISTRO Collaboration </p>
<p>
We present five technical validations of SCUBA-2/POL-2 polarimetry on the James Clerk Maxwell Telescope at 450 and 850 μm, using 174 archival fields in 69 molecular cloud regions and subsets appropriate to each test. Extended-source calibration avoids a deterministic shift of Δβ=-1.05 in the dust emissivity index β that would result from calibrating extended emission using peak brightness. We modeled Stokes I noise with additive and signal-dependent components. The measured median ratio of surface-brightness noise at 450/850 μm is 25. Independent tests of Stokes Q,U noise support approximately equal additive variances and weak error correlations, providing a basis for propagating polarization uncertainties. Corrections determined for individual fields reduce additive zero-level offsets of order 1 mJy arcsec^-2 in the 450 μm Stokes Q and U intensities. The three-step regression leaves a median combined residual amplitude of 0.07 mJy arcsec^-2. Independent comparisons with SOFIA/HAWC+ at 154 and 214 μm support the correction in strongly affected fields. We matched the 450 μm maps to the modeled 850 μm beam and the common 12.6''-300'' range of angular scales. We compared three reductions of the same 450 μm observations across 30 sources. Taking the median angular residual relative to the final product for each source, we find that beam matching reduces the median over sources from 6.3&deg; to 4.7&deg;. After matching, 29 of the 30 sources have median residuals below 10&deg;. We also quantified how residual Q/U offsets propagate in ratios of polarization fractions, differences in spectral indices, and polarization angles. These validations provide procedures and quantitative references for interpreting POL-2 measurements and combining them with polarimetry at other wavelengths.
</p>

### 92) [2610.28890](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28890v1): Not so Fast: Revised Calibration of SIDM Core Collapse

<p> Frank C. van den Bosch, Yarone M. Tokayer </p>
<p>
Self-interacting dark matter (SIDM) haloes undergo gravothermal evolution whose late-time behaviour is central to predictions for dense substructure and black-hole seed formation. We revisit the calibration of this evolution using a new spherical Direct Simulation Monte Carlo code, SphDSMC, which follows spherically symmetric SIDM systems without force softening and to substantially higher central densities than standard N-body simulations. We compare SphDSMC calculations with conventional N-body simulations and with one-dimensional gravothermal fluid models. We show that N-body results converge to SphDSMC only for softening lengths significantly smaller than those normally adopted for collisionless simulations, and that allowing multiple scatterings per particle per time step does not introduce significant errors when implemented appropriately. Calibrating the gravothermal fluid equations against SphDSMC, we find that the commonly used harmonic interpolation between the long- and short-mean-free-path conductivities is inaccurate. A gentler transition, however, yields excellent agreement for NFW haloes over a range of concentrations and truncation radii, with a single long-mean-free-path conductivity parameter. The revised calibration gives a long-mean-free-path collapse time that is somewhat shorter than previous estimates, but we show that collapse slows dramatically once the core becomes optically thick. We provide fitting functions for the late-time evolution and argue that black-hole seed formation by SIDM gravothermal collapse has generally been underestimated in duration, making it unlikely to explain the massive high-redshift black holes inferred from JWST observations without additional physics.
</p>

### 93) [2610.28743](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28743v1): Unveiling the correlation between the UV 2175 Å dust attenuation bump, PAH emission, and gas-phase metallicity at cosmic noon

<p> L. Arriscado, I. Shivaei, L. A. Boogaard, A. Mizener, D. Calzetti, P. G. Pérez-González, F. Rodríguez Montero, G. H. Rieke, Y. Zhu, A. Battisti, A. J. Bunker, E. Curtis-Lake, T. Díaz-Santos, Z. Ji, P. Rinaldi, B. Rodríguez del Pino, S. Tacchella, A. Varo-O'Ferrall, C. C. Williams, J. Witstok </p>
<p>
The UV 2175 Å attenuation bump is one of the most prominent yet poorly understood features of galaxy attenuation curves. Its main candidate carrier is polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon (PAH) molecules, which are more prevalent in high-metallicity environments. We present the first systematic study of the relationship between PAH abundance and the UV bump in 49 galaxies at cosmic noon (1.5 &lt; z &lt; 2.7), examining its dependence on stellar mass, star formation rate (SFR), and V-band optical depth. For 19 galaxies, we also investigate gas-phase metallicity using oxygen abundances. We measure the bump amplitude from VLT/MUSE spectroscopy and derive galaxy properties, including the PAH-to-dust mass fraction q_PAH, through spectral energy distribution fitting with Prospector, combining JWST/NIRCam and JWST/MIRI photometry. Oxygen abundances are derived from strong-line diagnostics using Keck/MOSFIRE and JWST/NIRSpec data. We find a positive correlation between bump amplitude E_bump and q_PAH (Spearman ρ= 0.60). UV bumps are rarely detected at low q_PAH, supporting PAHs as their primary carriers. Both E_bump and q_PAH correlate with stellar mass, oxygen abundance, and optical depth, and anticorrelate with specific SFR, suggesting that PAHs and the UV bump are suppressed in low-metallicity, dust-poor, and/or intensely star-forming environments. This is consistent with PAH destruction in environments with limited dust shielding and/or strong radiation fields. A principal component analysis of E_bump, q_PAH, optical depth, specific SFR, and oxygen abundance explains 97% of the variance, suggesting that previously reported correlations with stellar mass reflect an underlying dependence on metallicity. Finally, variations in dust geometry may explain the wide range of bump amplitudes (0-0.5 mag) at high q_PAH.
</p>

### 94) [2610.28738](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28738v1): A combined HST and JWST analysis of the stellar population in Draco II

<p> Axelle C. Raffin, Giacomo Cordoni, Helmut Jerjen, Gary S. Da Costa, Maria V. Legnardi, Anna F. Marino, Antonino P. Milone, Fabrizio Muratore </p>
<p>
Draco II (Dra II) is one of the faintest and most metal-poor Milky Way satellites known, yet its nature as either an ultra-faint dwarf galaxy or an extended globular cluster remains debated. We perform a photometric analysis of the stellar population of Dra II reducing deep multi-band observations from the Hubble Space Telescope (HST) and the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST). We select Dra II member stars based on their proper motions and photometric quality criteria. For the first time, optical and infrared photometry are combined to produce a stellar catalogue reaching 50 percent completeness at F814W = 26.5 mag (corresponding to the absolute magnitude M_F814W = 10.1 mag). The colour-magnitude diagram confirms that Dra II hosts an old stellar population with an age of 13.5_-1.5^+1.7 Gyr at a distance of 19.2±0.9 kpc. We measure an α-enhanced stellar population with a low mean metallicity of [Fe/H]_CaHK = -2.38 ± 0.17 dex and confirm the presence of an intrinsic metallicity spread of σ_[Fe/H] = 0.85 ± 0.13 dex. We identify 10 unresolved main-sequence binary stars in the HST catalogue with mass ratios q &gt; 0.5 in the mass range 0.43&lt;M_1/M<sub>&odot;</sub>&lt;0.71, corresponding to a binary fraction of f_bin^q&gt;0.5 = 0.21 ± 0.07. This value is higher than those typically observed in globular clusters and lies near the upper range measured in open clusters. The stellar mass function is best modelled by a single power law with a slope of 1.53±0.26 over the mass range 0.13&ndash;0.77 M<sub>&odot;</sub>. We find no evidence of mass segregation. Consistent results are obtained independently from the JWST catalogue. Combining these findings strongly suggests that Draco II is best classified as an ultra-faint dwarf galaxy rather than a globular cluster.
</p>

### 95) [2610.28735](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28735v1): Discovery of a Broad-Line Active Galactic Nucleus at z = 9.98

<p> Xingyao Cai, Akio K. Inoue, Masafusa Onoue, Suzuka Arai, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Rohan P. Naidu, Pascal A. Oesch, Jenny E. Greene, Ivo Labbe, Jorryt Matthee, Anna de Graaff, Yuma Sugahara, Ken Mawatari, Pratika Dayal, Xi Kang </p>
<p>
The James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) has uncovered a previously unexplored population of faint active galactic nuclei (AGNs) in the early Universe. We present MoM-BL-z10 at z=9.9837±0.0009, identified through a systematic search for broad-line AGNs at z≥6 using public JWST/NIRSpec PRISM spectra from the DAWN JWST Archive. The PRISM spectrum exhibits a broad component in Hβ at 5.35 microns. A narrow+broad Gaussian fit yields a broad Hβ detection at S/N = 3.87, with FWHM = 3480^+2040_-950 km s<sup>-1</sup> and L_Hβ,broad=1.25^+0.33_-0.31×10^42 erg s<sup>-1</sup>. The detection of [Ne III] &lambda;3870 and the high [Ne III]/[O II] and [O III]/Hβ ratios further support the AGN interpretation. Its rest-frame UV and optical continuum slopes, compact NIRCam morphology, and a tentative MIRI F770W detection suggest similarities to Little Red Dots (LRDs). The broad Hβ detection enables the first virial SMBH mass estimate at z~10. We derive M_BH=3.6^+5.8_-1.7×10^7 M<sub>&odot;</sub> and λ_Edd=0.11^+0.10_-0.07 using the standard single-epoch virial calibration and quasar bolometric correction. We also consider an alternative scenario adopting the LRD-specific bolometric correction and assuming λ_Edd=1, which yields M_BH=2.1^+0.6_-0.5×10^6 M<sub>&odot;</sub>. Under either scenario, the inferred SMBH mass at a cosmic age of only 470 Myr highlights the challenge of rapid BH growth in the early Universe, suggesting super-Eddington accretion from a stellar-mass seed or a more massive seed. As the highest-redshift broad-line AGN identified to date, MoM-BL-z10 provides a new observational benchmark for understanding the early growth of supermassive black holes.
</p>

### 96) [2610.28717](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28717v1): Chemical abundance patterns at z=1-7: Carbon, Nitrogen, Neon, Sulphur, and Argon abundances from JWST spectroscopy

<p> E. Cataldi, F. Belfiore, M. Curti, B. Moreschini, A. Marconi, F. Mannucci, G. Cresci, A. Feltre, M. Ginolfi, A. Amiri, M. Arnaboldi, S. Bhattacharya, E. Bertola, C. Bracci, M. Ceci, A. Chakraborty, Q. D'Amato, C. Kobayashi, C. Marconcini, R. Maiolino, L. Querci, M. Scialpi, L. Ulivi, G. Venturi, M. V. Zanchettin </p>
<p>
The relative abundances of chemical elements in the interstellar medium (ISM) trace the history of star formation and chemical enrichment in galaxies. We investigate the abundance patterns of carbon (C), nitrogen (N), neon (Ne), sulfur (S), and argon (Ar) in star-forming galaxies at z=1-7, combining ultra-deep JWST/NIRSpec observations from the MARTA survey with archival spectroscopy. We assemble 244 galaxies with [OIII]4363 detections and derive direct Te-based abundances. New empirical strong-line calibrations for N/O, Ne/O, S/O, and Ar/O extend the analysis to an additional 514 galaxies. We find distinct abundance-metallicity trends. N/O is enhanced by 0.18 dex in median relative to local galaxies, with a stronger enhancement at low metallicity. Direct measurements reveal decreasing S/O and Ar/O with increasing metallicity, while strong-line measurements suggest a flattening at higher metallicities, especially for S/O. We also identify a stellar-mass dependence in the O/Ar-Ar/H plane, with lower-mass galaxies exhibiting lower O/Ar at fixed Ar/H, consistent with differences in star-formation efficiency and delayed SNe Ia enrichment. Ne/O remains approximately constant up to 12+log(O/H)=8.2, with a mild increase at higher metallicities and no significant redshift evolution. C/O tentatively increases with metallicity, broadly following the local relation. Finally, a forward model shows that observational selection affects the abundance ratios differently: it mainly restricts the accessible metallicity range for Ne/O and Ar/O, but preferentially selects galaxies with enhanced N/O at low metallicity, with a weaker effect for S/O. These results provide new constraints on chemical enrichment at high redshift and highlight the importance of accounting for selection effects when interpreting abundance-metallicity trends.
</p>

### 97) [2610.28515](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28515v1): The clumpy fraction of galaxies at z=1-9 with JADES: evidence for a mass-dependent transition in clump origin

<p> Dávid Puskás, Sandro Tacchella, Phoebe Chapman, Floor van Donkelaar, Andrew J. Bunker, Courtney Carreira, A. Lola Danhaive, Francesco D'Eugenio, Qiao Duan, Patricia Iglesias-Navarro, Amanda Stoffers, Hannah Übler, Yongda Zhu, Mirko Curti, Emma Curtis-Lake, Kevin Hainline, Eiichi Egami, Daniel J. Eisenstein, Zhiyuan Ji, Benjamin D. Johnson, Roberto Maiolino, Marcia Rieke, Brant Robertson, Christina C. Williams </p>
<p>
We measure the fraction of clumpy galaxies (f_clumpy) over the redshift range z=1-9 and stellar masses log_10(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) ≥ 8 using JWST/NIRCam imaging from JADES. We define clumps as compact off-centre surface-brightness peaks with characteristic sizes of a few hundred parsecs, and develop a dendrogram-based clump-finding method with astrodendro to identify them directly in rest-frame UV, blue, and optical images. Clump-detection completeness is quantified through injection-recovery simulations, which we use to define (M<sub>&sext;</sub>, z)-dependent 50 per cent completeness-limited f_clumpy, alongside a probability-weighted completeness-corrected statistic for comparison. For the full sample, the blue and optical f_clumpy reach ≈55 per cent at z≈1-2 and decline to ≲5 per cent by z≈8-9, while the UV fraction remains higher at ≈12 per cent. At z≈1-2, f_clumpy increases strongly with stellar mass, from ≈35 per cent in the lowest-mass bin to ≈85 per cent at log_10(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)≥10. Most clumpy galaxies host only one or two robustly detected off-centre clumps, while higher-multiplicity systems occur preferentially in massive, strongly star-forming galaxies at low to intermediate redshift. Comparison with major merger rates suggests that mergers could account for a substantial fraction of low-mass (log_10(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)&lt;8.5), high-redshift clumpy galaxies, while the observed fractions in more massive systems (log_10(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)&gt;9) require an additional in situ clump-formation channel. Our results are consistent with a transition from merger-dominated clumpy morphologies at early times and low stellar masses to an increasing contribution from in situ clump-formation channels, such as disc instabilities, in more massive systems.
</p>

### 98) [2610.27949](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27949v1): Constraining Solid HNCO and Its Environment in Open JWST Data with Laboratory Spectra

<p> Ruslan Nakibov, Varvara Karteyeva, Katerina Borshcheva, Igor Petrashkevich, Mikhail Medvedev, Anton Vasyunin </p>
<p>
The presence of HNCO in the gaseous phase is well known for the interstellar medium, but the molecule is not detected yet in the solid phase. We report the first laboratory-confirmed identification of solid HNCO at 2260 cm^-1 in open JWST data using laboratory infrared spectra. For that, we grew binary mixtures of HNCO with main constituents of interstellar ice using ISEAge setup at 10 K: HNCO:H<sub>2</sub>O = 1:10, HNCO:CO = 1:10, HNCO:CO<sub>2</sub> = 1:10, HNCO:CH_3OH~=~1:10. We present transmission infrared spectra of pure HNCO and HNCO-bearing ices along with the corresponding band strengths at 10 K. The fitting of open JWST observational data with the laboratory reference spectra in a 10&ndash;23 K temperature range show that solid HNCO is predominantly located in the apolar layer rich in CO. The abundance of solid HNCO is approximately estimated as 0.04&ndash;0.15&percnt; relative to solid H<sub>2</sub>O. The positive correlation between HNCO and N<sub>2</sub>O suggests their co-formation via the chemical route that includes solid N<sub>2</sub>. The results are also supported by the numerical modeling of HNCO formation in protostellar objects with predicted abundance of 0.06&ndash;0.11&percnt;. We calculated the upper limits for CH_3CN toward two protostars and evaluated its abundance to solid water using the spectroscopic fitting and modeling.
</p>

### 99) [2610.27282](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27282v1): The Future of Gamma-Ray Bursts as Probes of the Epoch of Reionization in the Era of JWST and the Extremely Large Telescope

<p> H. M. Fausey, A. J. van der Horst, N. R. Tanvir, K. Wiersema </p>
<p>
The Epoch of Reionization (EoR) is a key era of cosmological history that remains poorly understood. An examination of the Ly-alpha damping wing of high-redshift GRBs can provide direct measurements of the fraction of neutral hydrogen (i.e., neutral fraction) of the IGM in the vicinity of the GRB host galaxy, in addition to the neutral column in the host along the sight-line to the GRB. The latter quantity can help infer the fraction of ionizing photons escaping the galaxy (i.e. escape fraction); the average escape fraction being a key input to EoR models. New and upcoming observatories such as the JWST and the Extremely Large Telescope (ELT) can obtain more robust estimates of the hydrogen column density and neutral fraction around high-redshift GRBs. We simulate spectra for the JWST NIRSpec and ELT HARMONI spectrographs using different GRB magnitudes and exposure times to determine their capabilities for measuring column densities and neutral fractions with the Ly-alpha damping wings of high-redshift GRBs. We find that both spectrographs will be highly effective for obtaining column density estimates. Despite the presence of telluric lines, we find that ELT HARMONI will be a powerful instrument for retrieving accurate neutral fraction estimates, but JWST NIRSpec will be vital for obtaining neutral fraction measurements for bright GRBs that fall in redshift ranges where ELT is less effective. Additionally, shorter JWST Target of Opportunity (ToO) follow-up times would greatly increase the accuracy of neutral fraction estimates obtained with NIRSpec spectra.
</p>

<p> William M. Baker, Francesco Valentino, Jens Hjorth, Gabriel Brammer, Ugnė Dudzevičiūtė, Kei Ito, Antonio Pensabene, Pengpei Zhu </p>
<p>
We present Leviathan, a sub-millimetre galaxy (SMG) spectroscopically confirmed at z=4.051 with JWST/NIRSpec. Modelling 31 bands of photometry from the rest-frame ultraviolet to the millimetre, we find a stellar mass of log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)=11.34^+0.15_-0.22, making Leviathan one of the most massive spectroscopically confirmed galaxies at z&gt;4, yet consistent with ΛCDM for a star-formation efficiency of approximately 20%. Although SMGs are often thought to be building the dense cores of compact quiescent galaxies, Leviathan is extended, with a rest-frame 0.9 μm half-light radius of 4.3 kpc. Spatially resolved SED fitting gives a half-mass radius of 3.1±0.4 kpc, consistent with its size in rest-frame 1.5 μm imaging. This is three times larger than quiescent galaxies of the same mass, but consistent with the size-mass relation of star-forming galaxies at 4&lt;z&lt;5 extrapolated to M<sub>&sext;</sub>=10^11.3M<sub>&odot;</sub>. Leviathan forms stars on the main sequence (approximately 400 M<sub>&odot;</sub> yr^-1) with dust spread across its disc. Both its stellar mass and dust are more extended than those of z≈3 SMGs. Alongside GN20.2b, which has a similar mass and star-formation rate but is three times smaller, these show that massive dusty galaxies at z≈4 can have very different stellar structures, and that not all have their stellar mass concentrated in a compact core.
</p>

### 101) [2610.26788](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26788v1): Low-mass and High-sSFR Hα Emitters at z ~ 2 in the ZFOURGE-COSMOS Field

<p> Jeung Yun, Kentaro Motohara, Kosuke Kushibiki, Nuo Chen, Masahiro Konishi, Hidenori Takahashi, Tomoya Yukino, Shuhei Koyama, Akiyoshi Tsujita, Ichi Tanaka, Hirofumi Okita, Tsuyoshi Terai </p>
<p>
We present a near-infrared spectroscopic follow-up study of seven Hα emitter candidates at z~2 in the ZFOURGE-COSMOS field, selected via the K_s-band flux excess method. Spectra obtained with Subaru/SWIMS confirm strong Hα emission line at z=2.06-2.49 for all the targets. Three galaxies are low-mass systems (log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) &lt; 9) with high specific SFRs, while four are high-mass systems (log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) &gt; 10) on the star-forming main sequence. The low-mass galaxies show consistent Hα flux measurements between the K_s-band flux excess method and the spectroscopy, whereas the high-mass galaxies show higher Hα flux inferred from the K_s-band flux excess than the spectroscopic measurement which may be attributed to their extended star formation distributions. The low-mass galaxies exhibit extreme [O III] &lambda;5007 emission with high rest-frame equivalent widths (EW_rest) ranging from 480 to 1064 \mathringA. They are characterized by high [O III]/Hβ ratios (&gt;4) and weak [O II] and [N II] emission lines, indicating high ionization parameters and low metallicity. The line ratios are comparable to those of star-forming galaxies at z ≳ 6 and their local analogs. JWST/NIRCam imaging reveals a clear mass-dependent color gradient: the low-mass galaxies present positive F115W-F277W radial gradients with blue cores, while the high-mass galaxies exhibit negative gradients with red cores. These results suggest distinct assembly pathways for low- and high-mass galaxies during the cosmic noon epoch.
</p>

### 102) [2610.25984](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25984v1): Gravitational Instability of Cold Axion Dark Matter Beyond Minimal Coupling

<p> Bryan Cordero-Patino, Álvaro Duenas-Vidal, Jorge Segovia </p>
<p>
Following recent observations of unexpectedly massive galaxies at high redshift by the James Webb Space Telescope, the revision of Dark Matter (DM) models &ndash;specifically regarding how DM couples to gravity&ndash; has become a central topic in Cosmology. In this paper, we develop an axion model for Cold Dark Matter (CDM) with a non-minimal coupling to gravity. From this framework, we evaluate the gravitational instability in the hydrodynamic limit. Our results demonstrate how gravitational instabilities depend on the non-minimal coupling parameter, providing a novel tool for aligning with the high-redshift observations from the James Webb Space Telescope.
</p>

### 103) [2610.25446](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25446v1): Connecting galaxy structure, quenching, and halo environments during cosmic noon

<p> Ziwen Zhang, Cheng Jia, Huiyuan Wang, Enci Wang, Yangyao Chen, Houjun Mo, Xiong Luo, Cheqiu Lyu, Jie Song </p>
<p>
The connection between galaxy structure, star formation activity, and large-scale environments provides important insights into the evolution of massive galaxies. However, the relative importance of different galaxy properties in predicting quenching and the environmental dependence of structurally distinct galaxy populations remain poorly constrained at cosmic noon (1≤ z&lt;3). In this work, we investigate massive galaxies with log M_*/M<sub>&odot;</sub>&gt;10.5 at 1≤ z&lt;3 using deep JWST/NIRCam imaging from the COSMOS-Web survey, combining measurements of central stellar mass surface density (Σ_1), star formation activity, and galaxy clustering. We find that Σ_1 is the most informative predictor of quenching among the considered properties, including stellar mass, effective radius, formation redshift, and local overdensity. The quenched fraction shows a strong dependence on Σ_1, increasing rapidly above a characteristic Σ_1. We further show that compact quenched galaxies exhibit a tendency toward stronger clustering than compact and extended star-forming galaxies, while compact and extended star-forming galaxies show similar clustering properties. These results highlight the importance of Σ_1 in tracing quenching and reveal that structural diversity among star-forming galaxies is not strongly reflected in their large-scale environments, providing new constraints on the interplay between galaxy structure, quenching, and halo environments during cosmic noon.
</p>

### 104) [2610.26969](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26969v1): Inferring the shapes of low-mass galaxies at z&gt;6 from FIREbox^HR. Where are the disks?

<p> Haojie Geng, Robert Feldmann, Jorge Moreno, Xuejian Shen, James Bullock </p>
<p>
Context. The three-dimensional shapes of galaxies provide clues to their formation and evolution. Inferring intrinsic shapes from projected sizes and axis ratios requires assumptions about the population distribution of shape parameters. Aims. We test commonly adopted assumptions in Bayesian shape inference for high-redshift galaxies. Methods. We analyze FIREboxHR galaxies with log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>)=6.5-9.5 at z~6-15. We measure intrinsic shapes from stellar particles using the minimum covariance determinant (MCD) estimator. We also create mock JWST/NIRCam images, measure half-light radii and axis ratios, and infer intrinsic distributions from the joint distribution of projected axis ratio q and size log a. Results. Low-mass galaxies are predominantly prolate, with a substantial spheroidal component. Intrinsic ellipticity E and triaxiality T are well described by Johnson SB distributions, while intrinsic size A is approximately log-normal. We therefore model (logit(E),logit(T),log A) with a multivariate normal distribution and allow general correlations. This model recovers the particle-measured shape distributions more faithfully than a multivariate normal model in (E,T,log A). Triaxiality-related parameters remain difficult to constrain from projected data without large samples or informative priors. Conclusions. Bayesian forward modeling can recover intrinsic shape distributions when its population model adequately describes the underlying galaxies. An unsuitable distribution can bias inferences about morphological evolution at high redshift.
</p>

### 105) [2610.24092](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24092v1): Can Blue UV-Bright Galaxies Drive Reionization at Cosmic Dawn? An M_UV=-19.6 Galaxy with f<sub>esc</sub>≈0.85 at z=9.244

<p> Yunjing Wu, Sijia Cai, Yongda Zhu, Masami Ouchi, Yuichi Harikane, Zheng Cai, Mengtao Tang, Khee-Gan Lee, John D. Silverman, Fengwu Sun, Xiaohui Fan, Zuyi Chen, Minami Nakane, Hiroto Yanagisawa, Angela Adamo, Fuyan Bian, Jaclyn B. Champagne, Dan Coe, Seiji Fujimoto, Enrico Garaldi, Tiger Yu-Yang Hsiao, Xiangyu Jin, Mingyu Li, Yongming Liang, Xiaojing Lin, Guan-Fu Liu, Junjie Mao, Masamune Oguri, Takafumi Tsukui, Feige Wang, Jinyi Yang </p>
<p>
We revisit EBG-1, a gravitationally lensed galaxy at z = 9.244, using newly obtained deep (14 hr) JWST/NIRSpec G235M (R ~ 1000) spectroscopy. EBG-1 is one of the bluest galaxies observed at z &gt; 9. Along with its remarkably blue continuum of β= -2.99 ± 0.15, we find very weak nebular emission from EBG-1. We obtain a 3σ upper limit of EW &lt; 8.4 Angstrom for C III], a marginal Hβ measurement (EW = 256.2 ± 122.2 Angstrom), and a 5σ detection of [O III] λ5007 (EW = 882.8 ± 180.6 Angstrom). These lines suggest an oxygen abundance of 12 + log(O/H) = 7.16 ± 0.25 (≃ 3&percnt; Z<sub>&odot;</sub>) and log(C/O) &lt; -0.88 (3σ), placing EBG-1 on the C/O-metallicity sequence traced by lower-redshift galaxies. To constrain the escape fraction (f<sub>esc</sub>), we compare the observed UV slope and emission-line equivalent widths with synthetic spectra generated using BEAGLE. The models favor f<sub>esc</sub> ≳ 0.75, with f<sub>esc</sub> ≈ 0.85 providing the best overall agreement with the observations. These results suggest that relatively UV-bright galaxies like EBG-1 can achieve the exceptionally high ionizing efficiencies supported by late reionization scenarios.
</p>

### 106) [2610.24078](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24078v4): Beyond the Disc&ndash;Spheroid Dichotomy at 0.6 &lt; z &lt; 8.0: Evidence for a Morphological Indeterminacy Regime from JWST

<p> Igor Kolesnikov, Reinaldo Ramos de Carvalho, Jeong Hwan Lee, Rodrigo Flores-Freitas, Vitor M. Sampaio, Luiz A. Silva-Lima </p>
<p>
Galaxy morphology is a fundamental tracer of galaxy assembly, but its characterization becomes increasingly uncertain in the early Universe. We investigate morphological classifications across different methodologies over the redshift range 0.6 &lt; z &lt; 8.0, using JWST observations of 15,567 galaxies with 6,099 synthetic galaxies from IllustrisTNG. To quantify morphology without relying on visual labels or prescribed light profiles, we use an unsupervised-supervised hybrid machine-learning approach based on the non-parametric MEGG system, which combines the second moment of the light distribution, Entropy, Gini, and Gradient Pattern Analysis. We find that parametric and non-parametric classifications remain broadly consistent at z &lt; 2.5, but diverge systematically at higher redshift. We identify this epoch as the Morphological Indeterminacy Regime (MIR), in which classical disc and spheroidal categories no longer provide a unique description of the galaxy population. The fraction of mixed-interpretation galaxies increases with redshift, reaching F_MIR ~ 0.55 at z &gt; 2.5. Cross-domain and projection-invariance tests show that the classifications are stable and insensitive to data set and viewing angle. In the interval 2.5 &lt; z &lt; 8.0, MIR galaxies occupy an intermediate stellar-mass regime (median; log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) ~ 9.7), between low-mass discs (median; log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) ~ 9.5) and massive spheroids (median; log(M<sub>&sext;</sub>/M<sub>&odot;</sub>) ~ 10.1), while irregular systems have stellar-mass distributions similar to those of MIR galaxies. We interpret these results as evidence that morphological indeterminacy is a characteristic feature of galaxy assembly in the early Universe, reflecting a population of dynamically young systems that have not yet settled into the classical Hubble sequence.
</p>

### 107) [2610.24069](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24069v1): Beyond the dust: JWST reveals extinction and ice content in local (U)LIRGs

<p> G. Speranza, M. Pereira-Santaella, I. García-Bernete, E. González-Alfonso, S. Arribas, I. Lamperti, M. Perna, A. Alonso-Herrero, L. Hermosa Muñoz, D. Rigopoulou, N. Thatte </p>
<p>
We investigate the spatially resolved extinction and ice column densities in four nearby interacting (U)LIRGs using JWST/NIRSpec integral field spectroscopy (R~2700; 0.97&ndash;5.14 μm). This wide range encompasses numerous (&gt;25) H recombination and H<sub>2</sub> ro-vibrational emission lines, as well as the prominent H<sub>2</sub>O (~3.02 μm) and CO<sub>2</sub> (~4.26 μm) ice absorption features. We develop three methods to derive phase-specific extinction for ionized (A_ K^ HII) and hot molecular gas (A_ K^ H<sub>2</sub>), and H<sub>2</sub>O and CO<sub>2</sub> ice column densities (N_ H<sub>2</sub>O and N_ CO<sub>2</sub>), exploiting H and H<sub>2</sub> emission lines and ice absorption features. Nuclear regions (0.2" radius) exhibit higher extinction and ice column densities than extended regions, with median A_ K^ HII and A_ K^ H<sub>2</sub> of 1.25 and 0.75 in the nuclei, compared to 0.61 and 0.50 in the extended regions. The ionized gas is more extincted than the hot molecular gas, with A_ K^ HII≈1.5 A_ K^ H<sub>2</sub>, while H<sub>2</sub>O ice column densities are comparable between phases. In some nuclei, continuum-derived N_ H<sub>2</sub>O reaches ~10^18.81 cm<sup>2</sup>, likely due to compact obscured components. These results suggest that ionized gas originates in more deeply embedded regions, whereas water ice envelopes both gas phases, with additional ice-rich material around the most obscured nuclei. The median N_ CO<sub>2</sub>/N_ H<sub>2</sub>O abundance ratio is ~0.10, a factor of 2&ndash;3 below typical Galactic values.
</p>

### 108) [2610.24064](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24064v2): Consistently Reproducing Early Galaxy Observations and 21-cm Signal during the Epoch of Reionization

<p> Michele Bianco, Sambit K. Giri, Arghyadeep Basu, Tommaso Boschi, Aurel Schneider, Enrico Garaldi, Garrelt Mellema </p>
<p>
In the standard cosmological model, galaxies forming within dark matter haloes are the primary drivers of the Epoch of Reionization (EoR). The complex physics of galaxy formation introduces scatter in the relation between halo mass and star-formation activity, with implications for the production and escape of ionising photons. In this work, we implement a stochastic source model in our cosmological radiative transfer simulations. We calibrate our model against the high-redshift ultraviolet luminosity function observed by JWST and observational constraints on Lyman-continuum leakage to constrain the ionising escape fraction. We find that stochastic star formation redistributes ionising emissivity toward lower-mass haloes, producing a more fragmented ionisation topology and delaying the growth of the largest ionised regions relative to the deterministic, observationally calibrated model. However, when the two models match total ionising photon production, their global neutral fractions and 21-cm power spectrum remain nearly indistinguishable, despite small differences in the morphology of individual ionised regions. Additionally, we establish a unified framework that lets us apply source models derived from computationally expensive hydrodynamical simulations (SPICE, FIRE-2 and Thesan) and investigate their impact on large-volume reionisation simulations, extending their prescriptions to scales of &gt;100 Mpc. We find that these prescriptions produce distinct ionisation topologies and 21-cm statistical signatures, even when their global reionisation histories are similar. Our results highlight the importance of combining physically motivated galaxy formation prescriptions from hydrodynamical simulations with large-volume radiative transfer simulations to interpret forthcoming 21-cm observations and constrain the sources of cosmic reionisation.
</p>

### 109) [2610.23806](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23806v2): Clustering of JWST LRDs. Implications for PBHs and other early-forming seeds

<p> Gert Hütsi </p>
<p>
We use the observed abundance and spatial clustering of JWST little red dots (LRDs) over z = 4&ndash;8 to confront two formation scenarios: the standard picture in which LRDs form in virialized dark-matter halos, and one in which they are seeded by a dynamically subdominant population of primordial black holes (PBHs). The halo formation scenario accounts for both observables naturally and without fine-tuning. In the PBH scenario, matching the observed LRD abundance requires a negligibly small PBH mass fraction, and a power-spectrum analysis shows that such a Poisson isocurvature component predicts a clustering signal far below what is observed. The same conclusion holds for any population of dynamically subdominant early-forming seeds, unless their initial clustering is finely tuned or their activity is tied to massive host halos. We conclude that the spatial clustering of JWST LRDs strongly favours the standard halo formation picture and disfavours PBHs, or similar early-forming seeds, as a randomly activated LRD population; PBHs remain viable as seeds only if their activity is governed by massive host halos, in which case clustering alone cannot distinguish them from astrophysical seeds.
</p>

### 110) [2610.22766](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22766v1): Dust in Galaxies at z=10-15: Attenuation and Re-emission Predictions from the FirstLight Simulations

<p> Yurina Nakazato, Akio K. Inoue, Rachel S. Somerville, Daisuke Toyouchi, Kosei Matsumoto, Daniel Ceverino </p>
<p>
Dust masses and grain properties in z&gt;10 galaxies remain poorly constrained, as JWST UV attenuation and ALMA dust re-emission are rarely modelled consistently. We perform post-processed 3D dust radiative transfer on 208 snapshots of 99 galaxies at z=10-15 from the FirstLight simulations (M_*=10^6.5-10^9.5 M<sub>&odot;</sub>), applying twelve dust models per snapshot (2496 runs): four dust-to-metal (DTM) prescriptions and three grain size distributions. Metallicity-dependent DTM models reproduce ALMA dust-mass upper limits at z≳10 and detections at z~7, as dust-to-stellar mass ratios rise sharply near Z~0.2 Z<sub>&odot;</sub>, typically at M_*≳10^8.5 M<sub>&odot;</sub>, where grain growth by metal accretion becomes dominant. A constant DTM of 0.4 instead violates more than half of the upper limits. For dust attenuation, SMC-like dust is favored by the observed M_UV-β_UV relation. The observed UV slopes (β_UV≃-2.3) favor small grains (a≲λ_UV/2π≃0.025 μm) producing steep UV extinction, together with a low abundance of carbonaceous grains or PAHs responsible for the 2175 \mathringA bump, consistent with silicate-rich dust. Large-grain stellar dust (a_0=0.1 μm) instead yields nearly grey extinction and UV slopes close to the intrinsic value (β_UV,0≃-2.6). SMC and MW attenuation curves flatten at A_V≳0.3, showing that relatively flat attenuation does not necessarily imply large grains. For dust re-emission, dust peak temperatures reach 60-80 K, exceeding the 40-50 K commonly assumed in ALMA dust-mass estimates. Predicted IR continuum flux increases with UV luminosity; at current ALMA sensitivities (10-20 μJy), detections generally require M_UV≲-21.5. Roman wide-field surveys may identify such rare UV-bright galaxies.
</p>

### 111) [2610.21513](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21513v1): Probing cold gas and dust in quiescent galaxies across cosmic time

<p> Anita Zanella, Sirio Belli, Belen Alcalde Pampliega, Manuel Aravena, George Bendo, Rachel Bezanson, Stefano Carniani, Francoise Combes, Pierre Cox, Anna de Graaff, Daniel Espada, Andreas L. Faisst, K. Decker French, Jonathan Freundlich, Mahmoud Hamed, Zhiyuan Ji, Gareth C. Jones, Vanshika Kansal, Adarsh Kumar, Andrea Lapi, Ivanna Langan, Umberto Maio, Alessia Moretti, Dieu D. Nguyen, Mamta Pandey-Pommier, Gergö Popping, Prachi Prajapati, Guillermo Quintana-Lacaci, Pierluigi Rinaldi, Raghvendra Sahai, Justin Spilker, Sandro Tacchella, Sabine Thater, Francesco Valentino, Livia Vallini, Jacco van Loon, Enrico Veraldi, Christina C. Williams </p>
<p>
The discovery of massive quiescent galaxies at high redshift by JWST has revealed that star formation can cease rapidly in the early Universe, yet the physical mechanisms responsible for quenching remain unknown. A key missing element is the systematic measurement in statistically representative quiescent samples of the cold gas and dust content, which are crucial for distinguishing quenching mechanisms and evolutionary pathways. Such observations are currently not feasible because state-of-the-art sub-millimeter facilities (e.g., ALMA) cannot routinely detect the faint molecular gas and dust reservoirs of quiescent galaxies, nor pinpoint outflowing molecular gas. A next-generation facility (ALMA2040) capable of providing transformative access to the cold interstellar medium of quiescent galaxies across redshift is therefore essential. In particular, it will allow the community to probe the molecular gas content of quiescent galaxies at cosmic noon and beyond by targeting the CO, [CI], and [CII] emission lines, as well as unveiling the physical conditions of the cold interstellar medium by constraining the CO spectral line energy distribution. It will also reveal the dust content of the passive population, a crucial ingredient to constrain the different quenching mechanisms. Finally, it will enable probing the molecular gas kinematics and identify molecular outflows driven by supermassive black holes. When combined with the rich legacy of JWST, ELT, and SKAO surveys, ALMA2040 will deliver a comprehensive, multi-phase view of feedback and quenching, allowing us to establish when, how, and why galaxies shut down their star formation across cosmic time.
</p>

### 112) [2610.21460](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21460v1): Dust at cosmic dawn: unveiling the onset of dust and obscured star formation at z≳8

<p> Hiddo Algera, Tom Bakx, Manuel Aravena, Leindert Boogaard, Karina Caputi, Stefano Carniani, Pierre Cox, Elisabete da Cunha, Miroslava Dessauges-Zavadsky, Tanio Diaz Santos, Andreas Faisst, Andrea Ferrara, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Carlotta Gruppioni, Mahmoud Hamed, Jacqueline Hodge, Ryota Ikeda, Hanae Inami, Adarsh Kumar, Andrea Lapi, Daizhong Liu, Umberto Maio, Dieu Nguyen, Omima Osman, Andrea Pallottini, Gergö Popping, Francesco Salvestrini, Irene Shivaei, Laura Sommovigo, Justin Spilker, Livia Vallini, Julie Wardlow, Joris Witstok, Mengyuan Xiao </p>
<p>
Although less than 1 percent of the baryonic mass budget of a galaxy, cosmic dust profoundly alters the multi-wavelength appearance of galaxies across cosmic time. The (sub)millimeter wavelength regime is uniquely capable of weighing these dust reservoirs, and revealing the total extent of galaxy evolution that remains hidden behind them. With current facilities, however, doing so at z &gt; 8 is largely out of reach. ALMA2040, a next-generation (sub)millimeter interferometer, is set to change this. Through an order-of-magnitude increase in sensitivity compared to ALMA, ALMA2040 is a transformational facility that will be able to routinely detect dust emission from galaxies at z &gt; 8 and, through simultaneous multi-band characterization, accurately measure their dust masses, temperatures, and infrared luminosities. Combined with ancillary metallicity measurements &ndash; including from the far-infrared lines detected by ALMA2040 itself &ndash; this will establish the relative roles of supernovae and grain growth in building up dust in the earliest galaxies. Simultaneously, ALMA2040 will measure the fraction of star formation that is obscured by dust at cosmic dawn, and thereby the extent to which rest-UV and optical surveys miss early galaxy build-up. Without a 10× upgrade to ALMA &ndash; comparable to the leap from the VLT to the ELT &ndash; we will not be able to capitalize on the momentous advances enabled by JWST, and will remain blind to the cold and obscured component of galaxy formation in the z &gt; 8 Universe.
</p>

### 113) [2610.21232](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21232v1): Are High-EW LAEs good analogs for the earliest galaxies? A study on the ionizing conditions and gas properties of LAEs at 2≲ z≲7

<p> Lena Pless, Themiya Nanayakkara, Karl Glazebrook, Michael Maseda, Jarle Brinchmann, Tanya Urrutia </p>
<p>
Lyman-α emitters (LAEs) are frequently proposed as analogs of the low-mass, metal-poor galaxies thought to dominate cosmic reionization. In particular, galaxies with large Lyα equivalent widths (EWs) are expected to have not experienced significant chemical enrichment. We test this hypothesis using a sample of 88 LAEs at 2 ≲ z≲ 7, selected from MUSE observations that are undetected in deep HST imaging and subsequently followed up with JWST/NIRSpec spectroscopy. Despite the unprecedented sensitivity of JWST, only 50 of the LAEs exhibit secure counterparts in available deep NIRCam imaging reaching typical 3σ depths of m_AB~30.3 mag, highlighting the intrinsically faint nature of the population. Gas-phase metallicities are inferred by fitting optical emission lines with a grid of BPASS+cloudy photoionization models, while stellar masses are derived from NIRCam photometry through SED fitting. The final sample with secure stellar masses and gas-phase metallicities encompasses only 16 LAEs, occupying regions of standard emission-line diagnostic diagrams consistent with low-metallicity star-forming systems. Although the strong-line metallicity diagnostic R3 appears to decrease with increasing EW, we find no compelling evidence for a strong monotonic relation between EW and gas-phase metallicity when metallicities are inferred through full emission-line fitting. However, galaxies with stronger Lyα emission tend to lie below the average mass-metallicity relation at fixed stellar masses, suggesting that they are preferentially less chemically evolved than typical galaxies of the same mass. We conclude that high-EW LAEs provide useful, though imperfect, analogs of the galaxies thought to drive cosmic reionization, and combining EW with stellar mass may provide a more effective route for identifying the most chemically primitive galaxies.
</p>

### 114) [2610.21205](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21205v1): A machine-learning-driven dataset of 144,111 polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon infrared spectra for interpreting aromatic infrared bands

<p> Xinghong Mai, Zu-Jia Lu, Zhao Wang </p>
<p>
The interpretation of interstellar aromatic infrared bands (AIBs) relies on reference spectral libraries, but the chemical space covered by existing databases remains incomplete relative to the vast structural diversity of polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) expected in the interstellar medium. We present a large-scale, high-throughput PAH infrared (IR) spectral database that complements existing reference libraries by providing significantly more continuous coverage of the PAH chemical space for small to medium-sized PAHs. We used a machine-learning double-harmonic (ML-DH) framework that integrates neural network force fields with electronic dipole models. This framework achieves quantum-chemical accuracy at a fraction of the computational cost. It has been validated against independent density functional theory calculations and benchmark spectral databases. The resulting dataset comprises 144,111 IR spectra for 48,037 closed-shell, even-carbon benzenoid PAH structures across neutral, cationic, and anionic charge states, complete with per-spectrum uncertainty estimates. The spectral library, optimized geometries, and model code are publicly available. They can be used to complement existing libraries for interpreting and modeling JWST AIB observations and those from future IR missions.
</p>

### 115) [2610.20948](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20948v1): JWST reveals a highly ionized nebula powered by an ultraluminous X-ray source with a massive black hole in the metal-poor galaxy Holmberg II

<p> F. A. Fogantini, P. Zeidler, L. M. Oskinova, T. Bao, W. -R. Hamann, P. Marchant, D. M. -A. Meyer, D. Pauli, S. Reyero Serantes, A. A. C. Sander, T. Shenar, R. Soria </p>
<p>
Recent James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) observations have revealed fine-structure lines of ions with ionization potentials ≳50 eV, such as [Ne \sc v] and [O \sc iv], in nebular spectra of some low-metallicity galaxies, raising questions about the nature of the ionizing sources. Ultraluminous X-ray sources (ULXs) have been considered as potential contributors, but direct evidence linking individual ULXs to highly ionized nebulae is limited. Here we present JWST IFU spectroscopy of the nebula surrounding the template ULX Ho II X&ndash;1 in the low-metallicity galaxy Holmberg II, already known to emit HeII &lambda;4686 and [O \sc iv] 25.89 μm lines. We resolve the infrared nebula on spatial scales of ~15-25 pc and report the first detections of [Ne \sc v] and [Ne \sc vi] in this source. We find anisotropic ionizing conditions: emission from the highest-ionization species arises from compact regions offset from the ULX toward the previously detected radio knots, whereas lower-ionization species trace the wider nebula. The preferential alignment of the highest-ionization emission with the radio axis suggests anisotropic escape of the ionizing continuum and a physical connection with jet-ejection activity. Spectral lines across the nebula are narrow and consistent with the instrumental resolution, indicating kinematically quiescent gas and disfavoring shocks as the dominant ionization mechanism. Together, these results provide direct evidence linking an individual ULX to a highly ionized nebula in a low-metallicity galaxy. These JWST observations of Ho~II~X&ndash;1 show that an accreting black hole in a low-metallicity galaxy is responsible for the extreme ionization of the surrounding nebula on ~100 pc scales.
</p>

### 116) [2610.20733](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20733v2): The dawn of the first galaxies

<p> Livia Vallini, Tanio Diaz Santos, Belen Alcalde Pampliega, Hiddo Algera, Manuel Aravena, Cecilia Bacchini, Tom Bakx, Maruša Bradač, Leindert A. Boogaard, Karina I. Caputi, Stefano Carniani, Karin Cescon, Francoise Combes, Carla Cornil-Baïotto, Pierre Cox, Elisabete Da Cunha, Roberto Decarli, Miroslava Dessauges-Zavadsky, Andreas L. Faisst, Roman Fernandez Aranda, Andrea Ferrara, Jonathan Freundlich, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Seiji Fujimoto, Carlotta Gruppioni, Takuya Hashimoto, Natsuki Hayatsu, Kasper E. Heintz, Jacqueline Hodge, Ryota Ikeda, Gareth C. Jones, Mahsa Kohandel, Adarsh Kumar, Alvaro Labiano, Ivanna Langan, Andrea Lapi, Umberto Maio, Marcella Massardi, Dieu D. Nguyen, Andrea Pallottini, Mamta Pandey-Pommier, Miguel Pereira-Santaella, Gergö Popping, Francesca Pozzi, Prachi Prajapati, Dominik A. Riechers, Francesca Rizzo, Lucie Rowland, Sander Schouws, Irene Shivaei, Laura Sommovigo, Yuma Sugahara, Hannah R. Stacey, Sandro Tacchella, Eros Vanzella, Enrico Veraldi, Julie Wardlow, Joris Witstok, Anita Zanella </p>
<p>
Understanding how the first galaxies assembled their stellar mass at cosmic dawn is a central frontier of modern astrophysics. JWST has now revealed an unexpected population of ultraviolet-bright galaxies at z&gt;10, whose intense emission points towards a rapid stellar mass growth that challenges current models of early galaxy assembly. Yet, the cold gas fueling this growth remains hidden: current ALMA sensitivity restricts routine detection to only the brightest ionized-gas tracers such as [OIII] 88μm, while [CII] 158μm, a key coolant and gas-mass proxy of the neutral interstellar medium (ISM), is undetected beyond z~8. This leaves fundamental questions unanswered: how quickly primeval galaxies gathered cold gas and turned it into stars; what physical conditions (density, temperature, radiation field) characterized their ISM; how fast metals and dust built up; and how feedback regulated early gas assembly. Only a transformational sub-millimeter facility like ALMA2040 can address these questions, enabling systematic surveys of [CII] in the first galaxies and providing a representative census of gas fractions and depletion times. A simulated galaxy at z=11.3 shows that ALMA2040 not only will detect, but also spatially resolve its merging cold-gas structure in [CII] on ~200 pc scales in 5 hours (below 100 pc in 10 hours). ALMA2040 will also spectrally resolve multiple lines simultaneously, akin to integral-field spectroscopy, providing the key diagnostics needed to break density-metallicity-radiation-field degeneracies and map the multi-phase ISM of z&gt;10 galaxies. The combination of dramatically increased line sensitivity and wide instantaneous bandwidth will push the search for the first galaxies out to the earliest cosmic times, with [OIII] detectable up to z~20. ALMA2040 will thus be transformative for understanding how the first galaxies assembled at cosmic dawn.
</p>

### 117) [2610.20727](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20727v1): Has JWST Seen Everything? Hunting for "NIRCam-dark" Dusty Star-Forming Galaxies at z ≃ 6 - 9 with ALMA2040

<p> Fengwu Sun, Belén Alcalde Pampliega, Hiddo Algera, Manuel Aravena, Tom Bakx, Karina I. Caputi, Pierre Cox, Elisabete da Cunha, Andreas Faisst, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Carlotta Gruppioni, Mahmoud Hamed, Jacqueline Hodge, Patrick S. Kamieneski, Adarsh Kumar, Ivanna Langan, Andrea Lapi, Umberto Maio, Chiara Mazzucchelli, Dieu D. Nguyen, Mamta Pandey-Pommier, Dominik A. Riechers, Irene Shivaei, Sabine Thater </p>
<p>
JWST/NIRCam has recovered the stellar counterparts of many ALMA-detected, formerly HST-dark galaxies, yet ALMA has started identifying dusty star-forming galaxies (DSFGs) at z&gt;6 that remain extremely faint or undetected even with NIRCam. These "NIRCam-dark" DSFGs may trace a missing rapid-growth phase connecting UV-luminous galaxies at z&gt;10 to massive quiescent galaxies at z≳4. So far, the small, heterogeneously selected NIRCam-dark sample is insufficient to determine their abundance or role in the evolution of massive galaxies. We show that a blind 1.3-mm continuum survey with ALMA2040 can provide the first high-significance census of this elusive NIRCam-dark DSFG population across z≃ 6 - 9. We conduct sensitivity and survey-speed calculations requiring a 10σ detection of a NIRCam-dark DSFG with a dust-obscured star-formation rate of 60 M<sub>&odot;</sub> yr^-1 at z = 8. At a like-for-like 7.5-GHz normalization, ALMA2040 will reach the required 1.3-mm RMS of 21 μJy beam^-1 about 90 times faster than current ALMA. With 100 hr on-source, ALMA2040 can cover an area of ~0.5 deg<sup>2</sup> and detect &langle; N &rangle;~16 NIRCam-dark DSFGs over 7.5&lt;z&lt;9, assuming a fiducial number density n=3×10^-6 cMpc^-3. Dividing the survey among independent fields with deep JWST coverage would mitigate cosmic variance and enable immediate NIRCam, MIRI, and spectroscopic characterization of their environments. The ≥512-GHz instantaneous bandwidth expected for ALMA2040 will also detect [C II] line at a wide redshift range, supplementing the continuum discovery with redshift confirmation. Such a survey will test whether NIRCam-dark DSFGs trace a missing rapid-growth phase between the earliest UV-luminous galaxies at z&gt;10 and the rapid emergence of massive quiescent systems at z≳ 4.
</p>

### 118) [2610.20730](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20730v1): ALMA2040: Understanding the Early Assembly of Galaxies - The Formation of Star-Forming Clumps and their Role in Shaping Galaxy Evolution

<p> Anita Zanella, Andreas L. Faisst, Rodrigo Herrera-Camus, Emily Chien, Beleen Alcalde Pampliega, Manuel Aravena, Cecilia Bacchini, Tom Bakx, Andrea Bolamperti, Leindert A. Boogaard, Marusa Bradac, Carla Cornil-Baiotto, Pierre Cox, Miroslava Dessauges-Zavadsky, Tanio Diaz-Santos, Jeremy Fensch, Jonathan Freundlich, Mahsa Kohandel, Andrea Lapi, Umberto Maio, Juan Molina, Dieu D. Nguyen, Miguel Pereira-Santaella, Francesca Rizzo, Justin Spilker, Hannah Stacey, Livia Vallini, Enrico Veraldi </p>
<p>
Astrophysics seeks to explain how galaxies form and evolve through the interplay of stars, gas, and dust. Giant molecular clouds (GMCs), the main reservoirs and sites of star formation, link the cold interstellar medium to the birth of stars and stellar structures. Their properties reflect galactic disk conditions, and their evolution is shaped by stellar feedback and host-galaxy dynamics. Studying GMCs across cosmic time is therefore key to understanding how gas turns into stars and drives galaxy evolution. Hubble Space Telescope and James Webb Space Telescope observations reveal compact star-forming clumps in galaxies from Cosmic Noon to the Epoch of Reionization (z≈6), but their link to the molecular gas reservoir is still unclear. We do not yet know how GMC properties vary with redshift and environment, how molecular gas, dust, and young stars are arranged, or whether clumps form in unstable disks or disturbed systems. Addressing these questions requires spatially resolved observations of the cold interstellar medium at the scales probed by optical/near-infrared facilities. ALMA2040, a transformational next-generation sub-mm facility, will provide the sensitivity and angular resolution needed to detect individual GMCs in representative lensed and unlensed galaxies at z&gt;1. CO and [CII], complemented by dust continuum and molecular-gas kinematics, will measure the molecular gas reservoir, its physical conditions, and its dynamical state. Together with JWST and the ELT, ALMA2040 will connect cold gas, dust, and stellar structures across cosmic time. ALMA2040 will revolutionize our understanding of (i) the properties and evolution of GMCs and their connection to star-forming clumps, (ii) the spatial interplay between molecular gas, dust, and young stars, and (iii) the dynamics, survival, and formation of GMCs and stellar clumps.
</p>
